on behalf of the ppe club. madam vice-president, the freedom to provide services is of paramount importance to the european economy. indeed, services generate as much as 73% of european gdp and create as many as nine out of ten new jobs. on the other hand, only 6.5% of european gdp is generated by the cross-border provision of services. this means that the enormous economic potential lies precisely in the strengthening of cross-border service provision, which is the subject of this report. the internal market and its four freedoms are the basis for the functioning of the european union. unfortunately, the free movement of services is the least developed of the freedoms of movement in the internal market. the reason for this is not only the fact that the horizontal legislative framework for services was only created in 2006 with the adoption of the services directive, but also that the implementation of this directive was delayed in as many as 20 countries and we know what all the political debates were on that occasion both at local and national and at european level. scientific studies suggest that the hidden potential of the single market for services is as high as €297 billion. this is a huge amount of money that we can use to finance health, support small and medium-sized enterprises or invest in protecting the eu's external borders and boosting european competitiveness in times of crisis. we therefore want to send a clear message today that the timely and correct implementation of existing legislation is the key to completing the single market. in particular, we must work to remove unjustified national barriers that hamper business in the single market. and this report sends a clear message: protectionism has no place in the european union today. i would also like to stress, of course, that this report strikes a good balance between the freedom to provide services on the one hand and the protection of social rights on the other. i therefore call for sustained political engagement and comprehensive support at european, national and local level to enable the single market in services to reach its full potential.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. since the pandemic began, many people have asked, "how can i help?" and i thank all of you who are helping to fight the pandemic, who are also helping to vaccinate, and who are thinking about how we can get as much vaccine as quickly as possible that is safe. but there are others who ask themselves, above all, who can i blame for black peter? who is to blame? who can i blame? that's not gonna help us. the eu-bashing is really out of place here. but i also say to the two speakers before me that pharmaceutical bashing is also out of place. if the attitude that mr botenga has shown had been the attitude of the negotiators, we would not have had a vaccine at all today. we have to work together, and i think that's a little short-sighted in the debate sometimes. the second point that concerns me very much is this new mutation from britain. thank god the vaccine seems to be working, but right now we just have to follow the rules even more strictly. that's why i'm wearing an ffp2 mask in my speech, which is not pretty, but it's better for all of us, and we need to do that now. and we need to have better, more effective controls at the border. there is a serious debate, ladies and gentlemen, about whether lorry drivers coming from the uk should be allowed to bring their sandwich, but there are no systematic pcr tests when entering the european union. we must protect our citizens better!

the commission has therefore decided to initiate the formal investigation procedure provided for in article 108 (2) of regulation (eu) no 1306/2013. it's a way of making any death from any cause without a medical cause of death being established a death from covid-19 within 28 days of a positive pcr test. so it's not being determined whether the person had covid-19 in the first place or whether their death had anything to do with covid-19. in order to facilitate the determination of damages to the individuals or families of the deceased, we must now apply the same standard. any death within 28 days of vaccination must be considered as a death by vaccination.

acting president of the council. mr president, i would first like to remind you that i am speaking here on behalf of the council and not as the representative of the portuguese state. i will say this, however: allegations of political interference in the appointment of the portuguese prosecutor have been heard in this house. i would like to emphasize that the decision to appoint him was based on the opinion of the superior council of the public prosecutor's office, a judicial body, independent of the executive, provided for in the portuguese constitution, composed of nineteen members, most of whom are prosecutors, but also including members appointed by the assembly of the republic. this council gave prosecutor josé guerra the highest ranking. i would stress that the candidates were also heard in the portuguese parliament in full transparency. this appointment was independent. furthermore, it is important to remember what i said at the outset. in accordance with the applicable rules, the ranking indicating the order of preference of the selection committee does not bind the council, which has full discretion in this matter. in the portuguese case, the order established by the superior council of the public prosecutor's office was maintained, in which there was no political interference. the commission notes that the commission has not provided any information on the amount of the compensation for the period from 1 january 2017 to 31 december 2018. this letter was examined by the council together with the reasoned opinion of the selection committee on the portuguese nominees. unfortunately, it contains two lapses and let me clarify what those two lapses were. the letter refers to prosecutor josé guerra, using the courtesy title of deputy attorney general, although he is, in fact, the attorney general of the republic. this reference is merely an identification element and was in no way intended to enhance their qualifications or to serve as a criterion of preference. the council assessed the experience of mr josé guerra not on the basis of his professional category but on the basis of his experience of more than three decades in the public prosecutor's office, including twelve years in eurojust in various capacities. the letter also states that the public prosecutor was involved in the investigation and trial stages of the ugt case, although in fact he only participated in the trial stage. the commission notes that the commission has not provided any information on the number of cases pending before the court of justice in the member states. these lapses, although regrettable, did not influence the council's assessment of the merits and professional qualifications of the portuguese candidates, as is evident from the letters sent to the unsuccessful candidates. moreover, the curriculum vitae of mr josé guerra was sent to the council at the beginning of this procedure and is completely correct, not containing any of the inaccuracies mentioned. i hope that these clarifications have dispelled any doubts about portugal's commitment to the european public prosecutor's office in a spirit of full transparency, but also about the commitment of the presidency of the council and the council to this enhanced cooperation project, which is as important as this one in protecting the financial interests of the union. this in writing. in order to be selected and appointed, european prosecutors must satisfy the conditions of independence and meritocracy, which are the main guarantee for the proper performance of their duties. the proceedings should have been held beyond any doubt or suspicion, but they were not. for some appointments the council decided without following the recommendations and rankings of the european selection committee. this, while permissible, has raised questions which call for clarity and maximum transparency in the decisions taken. the field must be cleared of any shadow of improper political interference. we cannot allow the european public prosecutor's office to be the subject of controversy, as was the case with the appointment of the european prosecutor in portugal. the european parliament, the council and the commission must protect its crucial role from any undue influence. it was set up to fight fraud, corruption and crimes against the eu's financial interests, a mission which is fundamental to the credibility of the european project itself.

the next item is the commission statement on earthquake mitigation in croatia (2021/2504 ((rsp)). members are informed that, again, no requested speeches or blue card questions will be allowed in the debate during this part-session. i would also point out that the speakers can be reached remotely from the liaison offices of the member states.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i’m taking this point on behalf of my colleague, elisa ferreira, the commissioner responsible for cohesion and reforms. few events have the capacity to shake us to our very core like an earthquake, and for anyone who has experienced it, you know how it feels when your whole world literally starts to crumble around you. your thoughts go instantly to your loved ones, especially the most vulnerable, who need your help to get safely out of the very building that, up to just a few short seconds ago, provided their sense of security. after the main shock come the aftershocks, which can be equally fatal as the remaining fragments fall. when these terrible moments have passed, attention must turn to rebuilding, because rebuild we must. i’m proud to say that croatia does not rebuild alone. i was in croatia when the earthquake hit. in the immediate aftermath, along with my fellow compatriots, i realised that croatia was not alone. the solidarity shown in response to croatia’s time of need was again outstanding. i take this opportunity to thank this house for its support and each and every member state that offered immediate assistance in the form of much-needed housing containers, winterised tents, sleeping bags, beds and electrical heaters. with my colleague commissioner janez lenarčič, i visited petrinja and glina with the croatian deputy prime minister to assess first-hand the most immediate assistance needed by the people and local authorities. of course, lives are the most precious of all, and it saddens me to report that eight people lost their lives and many were injured. on behalf of the european commission and of our european union, i take this opportunity to send their families and loved ones our deepest condolences. to those injured and recovering from the effects of the earthquake, we stand beside you. poštovana predsjedavajuća, u rezoluciji ovog parlamenta evidentna je zabrinutost oko moguće daljnje depopulacije pogođenih područja a sve kao rezultat ekonomskih i socijalnih posljedica potresa. kao potpredsjednica za demokraciju i demografiju, dijelim vašu zabrinutost. rezolucija s pravom skreće pozornost ne samo na ekonomsku i strukturnu stranu oporavka nego i na psihološke učinke koje ovakav događaj može imati. nacionalne, regionalne i lokalne vlasti u tome trebaju našu pomoć i podršku. i ovo je prilika za podsjetiti na članak 174. ugovora o funkcioniranju europske unije koji govori da će unija uvijek raditi na jačanju ekonomske, socijalne i teritorijalne kohezije a kako bi promovirala harmoničan razvoj te uvijek težila smanjenju razlika u razvoju pojedinih regija. taj članak podsjeća da posebnu pažnju treba posvetiti ruralnim područjima koja su izložena negativnim demografskim trendovima. potrebno je ovim se rukovoditi kada se pristupa obnovi ove regije, što znači da treba paralelno s obnovom stvarati uvjete za ostanak stanovništva, što je i glavna tema dugoročne vizije za ruralna područja koju će komisija uskoro usvojiti. nakon ove serije potresa dopustite mi da u tri točke izložim naš odgovor i podršku. prvo, europska podrška u neposrednoj opasnosti. drugo, europska podrška u naporima za oporavak a treće su sljedeći koraci u partnerstvu s hrvatskim vlastima. neposredno nakon potresa u zagrebu 22. ožujka aktiviran je mehanizam europske unije za civilnu zaštitu. komisije je također aktivirala službe za upravljanje izvanrednim situacijama copernicus kako bi pružile vitalne informacije i logističku potporu spašavanju i operacijama procjene šteta na terenu. podrška u okviru mehanizma europske unije za civilnu zaštitu i satelitskog sustava copernicus emergency mapping ponovno su aktivirane kada je 29. i 30. prosinca 2020. i 6. siječnja 2021. nekoliko potresa pogodilo područje središnje hrvatske. solidarnost europske unije odmah se pokazala te je većina zahtjeva ostvarena u roku od 24 sata od aktiviranja mehanizma. međutim, krizna podrška nije dovoljna pa me to dovodi do moje druge točke a to su europske potpore naporima za oporavak i obnovu. fond solidarnosti europske unije mobiliziran je prošle godine nakon potresa u zagrebu u ožujku. upravo je ovaj parlament odobrio 683 milijuna eur potpore. ovo je drugi najveći apsolutni iznos ikada uplaćen iz fonda solidarnosti. također je daleko najveći ikad u relativnom smislu, što predstavlja 1,26 % hrvatskog bruto društvenog proizvoda. to još jednom dokazuje važnost fonda solidarnosti europske unije kao jedinstvenog instrumenta unije, posebno dizajniranog za prirodne katastrofe. pružio je bitnu i pravodobnu pomoć hrvatskoj. fond solidarnosti podržava hrvatske vlasti i lokalno stanovništvo u oporavku osnovne infrastrukture, u operacijama čišćenja, u pružanju privremenog smještaja te u zaštiti kulturne baštine, ali još uvijek možemo učiniti više. i ovo me dovodi do treće točke a to su sljedeći koraci u partnerstvu s hrvatskim vlastima. nakon izvanrednih situacija uzrokovanih prirodnim nepogodama, neposredni prioritet je procjena štete koju je prouzročio potres a komisija je spremna u tome pomoći hrvatskim vlastima. na temelju procjene štete, fond solidarnosti europske unije može se ponovno pokrenuti kako bi odgovorio na zahtjev hrvatske za daljnju potporu naporima za oporavak i obnovu. šire gledano, moramo usvojiti sveobuhvatan pristup obnovi i oporavku od potresa, uključujući sve aktivnosti i imovinu na koju je potres utjecao. prirodne katastrofe nasm uvijek podsjete da se moramo usredotočiti na prevenciju, prilagodbu klimatskim promjenama i upravljanje rizicima. službe komisije već razgovaraju s nacionalnim vlastima. dostupni resursi u okviru kohezijske politike, instrumenta za oporavak i otpornost, europskog poljoprivrednog fonda za ruralni razvoj tu su kako bi pomogli građanima hrvatske. to uključuje ulaganje u mjere ublažavanja rizika od katastofa i mjere prilagodbe klimatskim promjenama, primjerice kao dio nacionalnog plana oporavka i otpornosti razvijana je flagship inicijativa za obnovu zgradu nakon potresa u zagrebu 22. ožujka prošle godine. komisija podržava one koji su najviše pogođeni kako bi se oporavili od ove štete, kako bi bili otporniji u budućnosti i kako bismo vratili hrvatsku na put održivog rasta i bolje budućnosti. hrvatska nije sama jer europa je svaki put bila tu u trenutku krize.

mr president, the european union brings together member states committed to the european idea and the european way of life, including democracy and the rule of law. however, political circumstances are such that in our immediate neighborhood we have a country that is not committed to the values described. while the fact that turkey has enjoyed candidate status for membership of the union since 1999 should mean that it shares our vision of fundamental values, especially the rule of law, this is unfortunately not the case. on the basis of the information provided by the turkish authorities, the detention of the opposition leader selahattin demirtaş in 2016 was considered a travesty of justice. the european court of human rights, in its december decision, unequivocally confirmed that demirtaşa's detention was not based on law and ordered his immediate release. ladies and gentlemen, i hereby express my regret that turkey, as our long-standing partner in maintaining international peace, has not respected the decisions of the duly constituted courts. when we add to this the increasingly aggressive behaviour in the eastern mediterranean, the increasingly difficult situation of christians, the persecution of political opponents and the links with the muslim brotherhood, we can unfortunately conclude that turkey is not moving forward but is moving further and further backwards. in other words, it is time for the european union to finally consider what kind of long-term cooperation it wants with turkey, given the frequent violations of the rule of law in this country.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. madam president, madam minister, mr executive vice-president, ladies and gentlemen, what we are doing today, adopting the european recovery plan, is historic. this is historic for three reasons. the first is that for the first time, we are investing together, united, in investments for the future. the second reason why this is historic is that we are connecting this recovery plan with the transformation of our economy and in particular with the green deal: 37% of these investments will be for the climate and 100% of the european money must not harm the environment and the climate. these are key results, which make a very big difference and which are why the united nations ranks this european recovery plan as the second greenest in the world. the third reason why this is historic is that we will pay back this recovery plan, not by making our citizens pay taxes, but by making those who do not pay taxes today pay taxes: the digital giants and the big polluters who import raw materials like steel into our markets, and do not pay taxes. all this we will decide formally today. that is why today's vote is historic.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on transport and tourism. – madam president, because i am also the shadow for the ecr group, i will try to touch on both issues – the opinion of the committee on transport and tourism and the political view from the group. i would like to thank all the colleagues who worked in the committee on transport and tourism and also on the main dossier on the rff for this final result, which we succeeded in reaching with the council. actually, as you may know, we normally always look forward to how it fits our plans, which is very important for member states, and also to make it flexible for member states. the final result was not so centralised and gave member states flexibility at the same time as keeping reasonable opportunities to control and govern from the eu dimension. it’s also crucial that we look at the grants part of the rff, that there should be a new approach from the member states, not only old plans which no longer fit into reality. it also very much touches the transport sector, where we have a huge opportunity to take the environment-friendly approach to mobility, but particularly taking into account that co2 emissions in transport are still very high in proportion to other branches of the economy. we should also have to check member states’ cooperation with our social partners and others in preparation for national plans for recovery and resilience, and also that it should cover all the envelopes. finally, in the transport sector, we have to remember that the ratification of own resources opens us to access for the commission to go for rff money to financial markets, to borrow this money. if the ratification of own resources is very slow, partly because some of the connecting europe facility and other dossiers are not yet finished and member states are very careful on these ratifications, that’s life.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, dear rapporteurs, honourable members, vice—president dombrovskis, thank you for having invited the council to take the floor on this very important debate, and allow me to start by congratulating the rapporteurs for their impressive work and to express the commitment of the portuguese presidency to ensure the swift adoption and implementation of this regulation. the covid-19 pandemic caused a once-in-a-generation crisis with deep social and economic impact, which will leave a mark on our societies for years to come. but the crisis also demonstrated that the european institutions could live up to the challenges, showing unity, solidarity, decisiveness, working together on an ambitious common tool. with the help of the european commission, the parliament and the council worked together to put forward the recovery and resilience facility that will help us to heal our wounds and stand back up stronger, ready for the future. this is europe at its best. the rrf is unprecedented in several ways. first, for its size: almost 40 percent of the mff and the next generation eu combined. second, for being the first eu financial instrument financed by common debt, even if temporary. third, for its capacity to provide much needed stimulus and the right incentives to address structural issues in an ambitious and forward—looking way. fourth, for its ambitious policy agenda on climate and digital transitions. and finally, for how quickly it was done. after intense and constructive trilogue discussions, we found political agreement on the text, which will be voted on today in this house and soon in the council. we are very happy to highlight the excellent cooperation between the presidency and the parliament to ensure a speedy entry into force of the rrf. now begins a new journey: a journey that the parliament, the council and the commission will make, together with each and every member state, towards stronger, greener, fairer and more digital and competitive societies. there is indeed a lot of work ahead of us. national parliaments need to continue the ratification process of the own resources decision. member states should present their national plans as soon as possible, and we all need to contribute to ensure their quality without slowing the process down. the regulation now reflects many elements that are important both for the parliament and the council, such as the six pillars, the european added value and the reference to the european pillar of social rights. also important is the link with invest eu, as well as the involvement with local authorities and civil society. the commission will have to properly take these elements into account in its swift assessment of the plans. the parliament will also play a key role in ensuring transparency and accountability through its structural dialogue with the commission. as for the portuguese presidency, i can only reiterate that we are committed to strengthening the eu through a swift and efficient implementation of the facility, ensuring a high degree of inter-institutional cooperation. we are also focusing on bringing the rrf to citizens and business as soon as possible, and we count on your support in this endeavour.
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, the recovery and resilience facility is a centrepiece of europe’s economic recovery plan next generation eu. it is key for ensuring successful, sustainable and inclusive recovery so that europe emerges stronger and more resilient from the crisis. the compromise reached by parliament and the council in december is a significant achievement. it is a balanced agreement that cements our determination to combine ambitious reforms and investments to secure our way out of this crisis in a lasting manner. the agreement is an important signal that the eu’s institutions are united at this time of crisis. let me highlight some of its main points. the compromise reflects the eu’s ambitions on the green and digital transitions. it sets a binding green target of 37% and a binding digital target of 20% at the level of the individual national recovery and resilience plans. it reinforces the green dimension, particularly by improving the methodology for climate tracking. it introduces new safeguards for digital measures that will help preserve europe’s security and strategic autonomy. while the discussions on governance were not easy, we found a compromise that ensures that the voice of parliament will be heard – and listened to – throughout the implementation of the facility, while respecting the constraints given by the institutional balance. the scope of the facility has been restructured in six pillars and member states are required to provide balanced recovery and resilience plans that respond to these policy areas of european relevance. they will also help to explain how their plans contribute to implementing the european pillar of social rights, on which the commission will shortly present an action plan. at the same time, the compromise preserves the anchor of the facility in the european semester, including the need for member states to address in their plans at least a significant subset of the challenges identified in the relevant country-specific recommendations. finally, the compromise agreement protects the eu’s financial instruments by making the approval of the plans contingent on adequate national control systems. now that almost all member states are preparing their plans at full speed, parliament’s endorsement of the agreement is very important. it is also essential that member states ratify the own resources decision as soon as possible so that the commission can start carrying out its funding operations. we need to put the facility into effect as quickly as we can. european money needs to start flowing to member states to help in their recovery, support their businesses and people, and re-start their economies in a direction that is sustainable and resilient. the commission is in constant contact with all member states to discuss the preparation of the plans. to date, there has been substantial progress and we have a relatively clear view of what 18 of the 27 member states intend to include in their recovery and resilience plans. however, there is still a lot of work ahead. even those member states whose plans are most advanced are now working on translating investment and reforms into operational milestones or on complying with other requirements of the regulation. we are there to help. developing high-quality national plans with a long-lasting impact will be key to the overall success of the facility. they will make the european union and its member states more resilient. for this to happen, we need to find a proper balance between reforms and investment. the plans also match our joint ambition to accelerate the green and digital transitions. member states need to include genuine green and digital reforms and investments to match the targets. the plans should not include a patchwork of small measures with little impact. indeed, they should reflect a clear strategy for embracing the twin transitions and implementing reforms in line with the country-specific recommendations. along with work on preparing and assessing the plans, the commission will also work on the delegated acts required under the regulation. first, the recovery and resilience scoreboard will be the main tool to inform the public on progress in implementing the facility across the six pillars. second, the commission should develop common performance indicators that will be published on the scoreboard. another important and related work-stream will be to create a methodology for reporting on social expenditure. in all of these areas, the commission will work constructively and transparently with parliament and the council to achieve the best outcome. we call on the honourable members to endorse the recovery and resilience facility so that member states can officially start submitting their plans for the facility and to put the facility into practical effect.
the committee on budgetary control, which is consultative, has adopted the opinion. the commission is not prepared to accept this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. first of all, the recovery and resilience facility is arguably the cornerstone of targeted and sustainable recovery to 'recover' from the pandemic, so to speak, but also to move our economy forward in a sustainable way. from the point of view of budgetary control, however, it is also important in this unique act of solidarity that we first of all know where the money is going, to whom the money is actually flowing, whether it is also distributed fairly and according to the objectives actually planned. the european parliament has negotiated an excellent basis and it is now up to the member states to make the best of it. but we also want to know, in terms of fairness of distribution, whether the imbalances that have existed in some member states will continue, whether only a few will benefit or whether jobs will actually be created sustainably for a wide range of people. and we also want to know that the money is not being used for purposes such as the absorption of regular and recurring expenditure in national budgets. we want this fund to be used for what it is intended to be used for, for the future of europe.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on regional development. madam president, colleagues, the current pandemic has taken the world by surprise. the best response has been the recovery and resilience facility, providing large-scale financial support for public investment and reforms that increase the resilience of national economies and better prepare them for the future. this is €672.5 billion of financial support, the core of the eu's next generation recovery facility. i thank all the rapporteurs, and as rapporteur on behalf of the committee on regional development, i want to thank all those who have accepted that the amount available for the grant support, 60% of this amount will be accessed by the member states by the end of 2024. i think it is also important for the member states and flexibility in terms of macro-financial conditions is important. of course, as we all know, the mechanism is to complement the amounts that member states have available for the 2021-2027 financial period, and the support will only be provided on the basis of plans drawn up by each national government separately, plans whereby member states will have to demonstrate that the money will go ... (president interrupts) ... where it is most needed ... (president withdraws the speaker).
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, mr vice-president of the european commission, madam secretary of state, it is urgent that the €672.5 billion made available by this recovery and resilience mechanism reaches the economy quickly and is used to modernise our countries. and we can't afford to lose this opportunity. madam secretary of state, countries like portugal should use this instrument, for example, to create an instrument for the solvency of small and mediumsized enterprises. do not delay this process any longer. and that leaves one question: when do you expect the decision on own resources to be ratified by all the national parliaments? because without it, there is no possibility of having this european recovery and resilience mechanism. for the first time, the european commission will use a guarantee from the european union budget to make a loan to member states to promote investment, and the interest and amortisation of €312.5 billion for grants is borne by the european union budget. we must make good use of these financial resources, otherwise it will be impossible to repeat this demonstration of solidarity. and so i ask the vice-president of the european commission: will the european commission be paying attention? will it force member states to come up with good plans, to comply with the regulations? the fact is that there are countries which are not listening to local authorities, they are not listening to the regions or to the social partners, and without that listening and that involvement there will be no approval, no positive opinion, from either parliament or the commission. i would like to end with a request: please do not waste any more time and this is a message to the member states. the economy needs it. small and mediumsized enterprises are in a very difficult situation. hurry up! it's time to move on and act fast.
the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the european union is able to respond to the crisis caused by the coronavirus. i would like to recall the resolutions that this parliament adopted in april and especially in may, with the broad support of many parliamentary groups, calling for a fiscal response of solidarity, an ambitious fiscal response and an investment programme that could contribute to the recovery process after the months of lockdown and these difficult months of fighting the pandemic. and i also wanted to assert the role of this parliament, supporting the commission's initial proposal and demanding that the european council validate it at that difficult summit in july, and the work of the last few months to improve the multiannual financial framework and to improve, of course, the regulation as well. because parliament has made this text into a self-contained proposal, a proposal which clearly sets out the investment priorities for all the countries of the european union, for the union as a whole, and those six pillars which the regulation establishes, to help, to shed light and to guide the work which the member states now have to do in implementing the various recovery programmes. this parliament has also ensured that budgetary conditionality remains pending that future revision of the tax rules and that, in addition, the recovery plans are supervised by european citizens, which is none other than the supervision of this european parliament. so nine months of hard work and we now pass the responsibility mainly to the member states, who have to develop, who have to deliver on those national recovery and resilience plans, as i say, under the guidance of the european institutions. and to trust that this response, which is very different from the one europe made ten years ago, will help to open up a new horizon for europe as a whole.
on behalf of the green group, madam president, mr vice-president of the commission, madam president-in-office of the council, today we are giving final approval to the recovery fund regulation and it is certainly a historic day. we're going to launch the famous eurobonds, because that's what they are, eurobonds, shared debt to make transfers. the years of the troika are behind us, the years of savage austerity which caused us to suffer a much deeper financial crisis and to emerge from it later. somehow, all those who for years have been denouncing austerity in europe can sing victory. with a european central bank finally intervening in the debt markets and with financial instruments of shared debt to invest, the european union and the eurozone in particular are much better equipped today and the european project has much better prospects. there is much to be done, of course, and the risks are great. the biggest one, in my view, is the premature reintroduction of the stability and growth pact, which, if it is decided to do so, must be urgently reformed because it no longer serves to shorten the way and give perspective to fiscal policy at the level of the european union. we have achieved a great deal as a parliament in the negotiation of this regulation and i would like to highlight this: greater climate ambition, with a new methodology for calculating climate expenditure: with a no-harm principle, which will exclude the large part of polluting investments; with greater involvement of local and regional authorities in the management of the funds, also with new social objectives. i think the european parliament has done an excellent job and i want to congratulate it on that. it is now obviously up to us as a parliament to monitor the implementation of the instrument and we will of course be very demanding of the member states. they comply with the rules of climate spending; they comply with gender equality, which is something that we are concerned about in the execution of the plan as well. but we will also be demanding of the european commission and the kind of reforms, above all, that it will demand. there's a lot of work to do. of course, we must change the tax laws of many member states. after the openlux leak yesterday, i think we have a lot of work to do there. but above all, mr vice-president, we do not want to go back to the kind of anti-social reforms that have done so much damage to our economies. that is not the way to go, and neither the regulation nor the country-specific recommendations indicate that way. the way forward is to build more cohesive societies, to make investments and reforms that are socially just. finally, i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their excellent cooperation. today is a very important day for the european union and i sincerely believe that this house has lived up to the historic moment.
president. - the rapporteurs and other speakers have already spoken at length about the characteristics of this fund, which is absolutely necessary to get our economy back on track, as it is called. but i would like to present another approach in my consideration. i regard this fund as a new opportunity for the commission to steer this instrument, to ensure that the quality, sustainability and effectiveness of the projects are guaranteed. i hope that this opportunity will be taken, because the commission has missed the first and great opportunity to support that recovery - namely, to promote rapid and efficient vaccination throughout the union. don't get me wrong: i am fully in favour of european coordination. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the member states are able to comply with the directive. but it is equally disappointing that the commission has not given sufficient substance to this coordinating role. we have started too slowly, with all sorts of manoeuvres which have only increased the backlog and the uncertainty about contracts, with a confrontation with the pharmaceutical industry as an inevitable consequence. and along the way, some have managed to break the trust. despite all the explanations and excuses, it's not a very nice course. the commission set itself the objective of joining the european union in order to become a geopolitical player. that sounded like music to many of us back then. but with what has happened in the last few weeks, the opposite has been achieved. i feel that a great deal of respect and confidence has been lost, both from the international players, from the commission itself and, i dare say, from the heads of government.

mr president, the recovery fund we are approving today is a positive step. the greek members of the left support it, although we consider it completely inadequate. we support it because the issuing of common debt by the 27 with a commission guarantee implements the positive proposal for eurobonds, albeit with a long delay. we support it because the 390 billion euro in grants to the european south and the european east is a serious step forward in transitional payments. but it is completely inadequate because it was designed almost a year ago and in the meantime the intensity and duration of the pandemic, the scale of the crisis and the recession require more drastic additional measures. the left group calls for the recovery fund to be doubled from 750 billion to 1.5 trillion euros, so that we can really have a european 'bazooka'. extend the suspension of the stability pact until 2023 and replace it with a realistic cohesion and sustainable development pact. european debt relief and acceleration of national plans to put this money where it belongs, away from client networks and corruption. this money does not belong to governments and parties; it belongs to people and societies.

madam president, madam president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, we have tried to speed up the virus, but we can see that the virus is moving faster than our technologies. the result: where the first clinical trials of vaccines began, variants are multiplying and outperforming the efficacy we expected of vaccines. the mutations will continue. life is innovating faster than biotechnology. this shows the impasse of the current european vaccine strategy, which has relied heavily on new types of genetic vaccines. this means that more research will be needed on perhaps more traditional vaccines, to increase the immunity they can provoke, and above all to develop national health infrastructures. there is an urgent need to strengthen virus sequencing and develop rapid and reliable tests, including saliva tests, capable of identifying variants in order to better track their spread. madam president, in these circumstances, how do you propose to reorient the vaccination strategy in europe? finally, we talk a lot about transparency, but you still haven't published the contracts with pfizer and moderna. we also want data to be accessible at the clinical trial level, especially since these contracts are accessible in the us and not in europe.

member of the commission. – madam president, i thank the honourable members for the question and recommendations. i heard alarm about this power plant and about its risks and i can assure you that the commission will not spare any efforts to avoid any risks for our citizens from this project. we are as concerned as this parliament is. you raised many topics, among them the issue of electricity trade. the three baltic states decided already in august 2020 that commercial exchanges with electricity from belarus will be stopped as soon as the ostrovets nuclear power plant becomes operational, and commercial exchanges with belarus have effectively stopped on 3 november last year when the nuclear power plant was connected to the grid. we do not have evidence that would confirm that the electricity sold to the baltic market by russia would have been bought from belarus, but we are looking closely at all information available on trade flows and we will continue following the developments. on the other hand, physical flows are unavoidable as the three baltic states are still connected to the grid with russia and belarus. that is why we need the rest of this programme through the synchronisation process with the eu continental grid, and the commission is committed and contributes to it with eur one billion. moreover, the european council conclusions from december 2020 have raised the issue of eu-level measures to prohibit electricity from nuclear power plants from third countries which do not fulfil eu-recognised nuclear safety standards, and the commission services are carrying out analyses on whether obligations under international law and national prerogatives on nuclear energy leave scope for measures at eu level concerning electricity imports from third countries. the commission is also working closely with the baltic states to explore whether specific measures could be put in place in the meantime before synchronisation takes place in 2025. such measures include, for instance, robust investigations on the origin of electricity imported from third countries or defining a grid use tariff also for import flows. some of you also stressed the importance of implementing all the safety recommendations, and indeed the commission insists on that. yet the definition of key recommendations has not been made by the commission but by the european nuclear safety regulators group (ensreg), and i fully trust the expertise of ensreg on this matter. some of you also asked me if we have a second opinion other than our ensreg experts group. belarus is party to the relevant international atomic energy agency (iaea) nuclear safety conventions, including the convention on nuclear safety, and is bound by their requirements. belarus has recently hosted a iaea peer review mission and an integrated regulatory review services follow-up mission foreseen in the third quarter of this year. in any case, the commission will not stop making any efforts to ensure the nuclear safety of the ostrovets nuclear power plant based on the outcomes of this week’s peer review mission, and we know that a second mission must take place to look at all the other recommendations. we make every effort that is needed to ensure the continuation of the voluntary cooperation between belarus on nuclear safety. we will also continue to work on the follow-up to the december european council conclusions and to promote joint action of the baltic states and electricity trade issues, and on the progress of the synchronisation project. i count on your support on this.
in writing. today we are discussing the issue of the astravy nuclear power plant, which is important for the security of lithuania and neighbouring countries. its location is worrying, as the power plant is located about 50 km from vilnius and close to other eu countries such as poland, latvia, estonia. we're at a dead end. we could have avoided that. the failure to close down the efficient ignalina power plant was a mistake; furthermore, the lack of concrete action by the ec and lithuanian authorities in the past regarding the location of the power plant will also be to our disadvantage. this has accelerated belarus' decision to build a power plant in astrava with the aim of selling energy to lithuania. in a special statement in 2011, i warned and called on the commission to step up negotiations with belarus on the planned construction of a new power plant, to set up a bilateral working group to develop common high standards of nuclear safety and to agree on a secure future site for the plant. if my voice had been heard, the astravy power plant problem would probably not exist. what can we do today to reduce the risk to the public once the plant is up and running? the commission should start negotiations with belarus as a matter of urgency, preferably by march 2021, when the belarusian authorities plan to start commercial operation of the power plant, in order to encourage belarus to cooperate closely by suspending the start-up process until all recommendations resulting from the eu's most extreme tests have been implemented and all safety improvements have been implemented. the commission needs to review the existing legal and regulatory framework for the safety of nuclear installations in the eu and beyond, as safety at the astravyansk power plant is a priority.

member of the commission. – (inaudible) that we have at our disposal in the fight against the covid-19 virus is the face mask. today, specific requirements regarding the use of face masks have been introduced across the eu under the competence of each member state. however, with all of us now having to wear a face mask in certain settings, it is essential to ensure that everyone can find the mask that serves their needs, while remaining safe. in that respect, the recommendations issued by the european centre for disease prevention and control, as well as by the world health organization, are our trusted scientific references. the rules regarding the design, testing and placing on the market of surgical and protective masks are laid down at eu level respectively by the directives and regulations on medical devices and by the regulation of personal protective equipment. furthermore, we have seen the emergence of other types of masks. these new products, for which the manufacturer does not claim a corresponding intended protective or medical purpose, are covered by the general product safety directive that requires all products placed on the eu market to be safe. our aim is to ensure that safe and compliant products can be placed on the market to meet the needs of all users, including vulnerable groups that rely on visual communication. the world health organization estimates that over 5% of the world’s population has disabling hearing loss. being able to see the lips and facial expressions of the interlocutor is crucial for those who rely on visual communication, such as lip reading or using sign language. this imperative could at first appear to be difficult to reconcile with the barrier function that the masks have to provide. however, this is not the case. existing rules on medical devices, as well as the ppe legislation, are technologically neutral which means that they don’t prescribe specific technical solutions for the products. any design, any choice of materials or components is acceptable as long as the entire mask complies with the defined performance requirements and ensures an adequate level of protection according to the intended use defined by the manufacturer. i believe that the eu rules on ppe and on medical devices are sufficiently flexible to accommodate face masks of any design and materials, including transparent face masks, as long as they meet the same performance requirements as the more widely-used non-transparent masks. i would like to stress that all the relevant actors – manufacturers, standardisation and testing bodies, member states’ authorities and the commission – should work in close cooperation to ensure that face masks continue to be a useful and safe tool in the fight against the covid-19 virus and to preserve the users’ trust.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, colleagues, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i was wearing a mask that meets the standards, but my sign language interpreter could not translate me, and i did not understand the words of those wearing such masks. unfortunately, these traditional masks cause communication difficulties for nearly one million deaf and 51 million hard-of-hearing citizens in the european union. they can't see the face, the facial expressions, the articulation of the person speaking. a solution might be to wear a transparent mask so that the other person's articulation, facial expressions, are visible. there is currently no transparent mask approved on the european market, and many deaf and hard of hearing people use unapproved masks at their own risk. a good example from home: fear of not being able to communicate may be why many people stay at home. europe must therefore support this development and then approve it, and the masks developed, which are therefore transparent, must also help to ensure that they are distributed. this is the only way to guarantee full access and non-discrimination. my country, hungary, is leading the way in this matter. since january, face shields have been available at government offices for administrative purposes. communication becomes accessible to deaf and hard of hearing people, and the online videoconferencing service of kontakt plays a key role in this. this allows deaf and hard of hearing people to handle cases independently without the physical presence of sign language interpreters, thus reducing health risks.

madam chair, if there's one symbol of the coronavirus crisis, it's the mask. whether you should wear a mask really depends on who you listen to and who you trust. if we go back a few years, back in the second, third month when the koran came to europe, you had all kinds of authorities, presidents, prime ministers, from the croatian ones and, i don't know, to macron, who said if you don't have symptoms, if you're not sick, don't wear a mask. this was repeated by all the headquarters in europe, and then in the fifth month, the story turned around and the mask became mandatory, penalties were introduced, and we got to this current situation. so obviously, that point was taken up by someone else. so it's primarily a political decision and who the politician wants to listen to and what measures he wants to put in his own country. it is worth noting, for example, that the court, the constitutional court of bosnia and herzegovina, ruled that wearing a mask, therefore, is a violation of constitutional rights. unlike these experts in the headquarters who often came there on a political line, we have a whole series of world authorities, scientific papers that are skeptical about all of this, about the crisis, about the masks, about the vaccines, and even about whether this is a pandemic at all.
madam president, according to the world health organization, 5% of the world's population is dealing with hearing loss. many of them use sign language to communicate with their family and friends. so in order to communicate with their environment, they necessarily rely on facial expressions and facial recognition. epidemiological measures introduced to contain the pandemic require the wearing of protective masks covering part of the face. in this way, our fellow citizens with hearing impairments have a significantly reduced ability to communicate, and are even more exposed to social exclusion in these difficult times. to raise awareness of this problem, it is essential to promote the wearing of transparent masks made of transparent material that allow hearing-impaired people to communicate with their environment. unfortunately, such protective masks are many times more expensive than conventional opaque masks. i therefore call on the european institutions and all member states not only to promote the wearing of transparent masks but also to ensure that such masks are accessible to people with hearing impairments. i think the european union, which has the power to support and complement the action of the member states in the field of public health, should be involved financially. through the eu health programme, horizon and other budget lines, and of course through the standardisation already mentioned here. i also believe that this problem must be tackled in the new european disability strategy. finally, we must devote ourselves to combating the irregularity of masks, which is a very serious problem, as i have already said many times as a member of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. let us show once again european solidarity in action.

member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen of the house, i am particularly pleased to have a debate with you today on the duty of vigilance of companies throughout their value chains. the commission welcomes the work and report of the committee on legal affairs and the interest of the european parliament in these matters. i would like to congratulate and thank in particular the rapporteur, lara wolters, and the fictitious rapporteurs on this dossier with whom i have had the opportunity to exchange views on several occasions. we share the ambition of horizontal legislative action at european level to implement the union's commitments to a transition to a fairer and more sustainable economy in practice. we see a clear momentum across europe towards a legislative proposal, a sustainable corporate governance, and the commission will deliver on this. the objective of the commission is to foster a sustainable, fair and competitive eu economy as we recover from the current crisis. this initiative will be an important contribution to achieving those goals. the private and public sector should both contribute to achieve this common goal. in addition, it is important that we respect our values, both within and outside the union. we want to incentivise, outside europe, good practices that protect the environment in line with our international commitments and our efforts on climate change and environmental protection. similarly, eu companies should make all reasonable efforts to ensure that practices against human rights, labour and social rights that are not tolerated within the union do not take place in their supply chain. we want effective action: an enforcement mechanism to ensure that forced labour does not find a place in the value chain of eu companies. many eu companies are seriously committed to preventing adverse sustainability risks and impacts throughout their value chains, thereby contributing to addressing key societal challenges. let me insist on this. many companies are already showing us the way, and this is a field where we want to work with companies and to support them, notably by putting in place a strong level playing field at eu level. ample economic evidence shows that sustainable companies, those properly integrating the interests of their stakeholders including workers into their decision-making and paying attention to their environmental and social impact, often perform better, are more productive and more resilient. this should be europe's competitive edge. sustainable corporate governance is part of the overall transformation towards a more sustainable and inclusive economy, and it can contribute to companies' resilience and performance in the long term. companies should carry out sustainability due diligence proportionate to their capabilities, to mitigate social, human rights and environmental harm in their business operations and supply chains. this means that those facing bigger risks would need to do more, as due diligence is a proportionate tool. harmonised eu legislation would avoid the risk of fragmentation of the internal market. as you know, such fragmentation increases the cost for businesses and investors. due diligence rules under eu law would also bring legal certainty and as i said, a level playing field in the whole european union. it is something many businesses and citizens are calling for throughout the eu. the commission will present its legislative proposal on sustainable corporate governance later this year. we envisage a holistic approach where a corporate due diligence obligation is underpinned by directors' duty. we will also consider the complementarity work carried out by this parliament on the report prepared last year by mr pascal durand. specific attention will be given to the costs and burdens for companies, including indirect costs and, in particular, for smes. i can assure you that i will be very attentive to this issue. a range of possible solutions is being considered as part of the impact assessment the commission is currently conducting. this could range from limiting the scope of application according to the size and riskiness of the sector or companies, to adapting certain requirements to the size of the company, in line with the proportionality principle. we are also looking into possible targeted support, such as guidance, tools, funding or technological solutions for traceability. as supported by parliament in your draft report, we are looking to base the future of the due diligence obligation on existing international guidelines and commitments stemming from international agreements. as regards liability, we are assessing this issue as part of the preparatory work for our initiative, having in mind international standards and the need to ensure an effective enforcement mechanism in line with our objectives. let me also assure you that the commission will ensure alignment between this proposal and a review of the non-financial reporting directive, in order to avoid duplication of reporting obligations for companies. we will also ensure coordination and coherence with other complementary initiatives, such as the one on deforestation, in particular when it comes to the definition of 'deforestation-free'. in conclusion, the commission is currently in the process of finalising the results of the public consultation we conducted on this initiative, which closed on 8 february. we received no less than 470 000 contributions. an impact assessment is currently being prepared, and i thank you very much for your support for this initiative, for your attention today, and i am, of course, now looking forward to our debate.
the committee on development and cooperation has tabled a number of amendments. madam president, commissioner, i would first of all like to thank the rapporteur, mrs lara wolters, and the fictitious rapporteurs, the rapporteurs for opinions of the committee on development, whose report i shall try to summarize. the introduction of a duty of care instrument is essential to ensure respect for human rights, labour rights and environmental standards, both within and beyond the european union. as rapporteur, i would like to emphasise some of the key points in the opinion of the committee on development. i counted seven. the first is that a binding and harmonised framework at european level is absolutely necessary to ensure a level playing field for businesses. the second is that the obligation to respect and protect human rights, the environment and to avoid corruption risks must be integrated throughout global value chains, products, services and trade relations. third, the new legislation should apply to all sectors, all types of businesses across the european union, with the focus on risk. fourth, these mandatory due diligence processes must cover the activities of companies and their business relationships, including their supply and subcontracting chains. fifth, a comprehensive system of accountability including administrative, civil and criminal liability should be put in place to enforce the new legislation and ensure its application, including through sanctions of all kinds. sixth, it is essential that future legislation is fully aligned with existing legal obligations and standards at international and european level, and that it addresses all types of human rights violations. finally, the chapters on sustainable development and free trade agreements must become binding and enforceable. in conclusion, i hope that this motion for a resolution will be the basis for ambitious legislation. i thank once again all those involved in this crucial matter and wish you excellent legislative work, commissioner.

madam president, living in a globalised world means that we have the chance to share knowledge, cultures, to consume products that we would never have consumed without globalisation. but it also means that we are interdependent and that our behavior here has repercussions elsewhere. the hyper-globalisation we have witnessed in recent decades has also led to economic, social and environmental disruptions that are harmful to all. for example, it has allowed companies to relocate to countries with lower social, fiscal or environmental standards, leaving thousands of protected workers behind and exploiting workers who have no protection at all. having a smartphone, a computer or an electric car also means that mining is over-exploited in africa to the detriment of the people, because these rare metals are often not paid at their fair price. these examples show us that the globalisation we are boasting about, which we are benefiting from, also needs to be framed. just as we have done for the conflict minerals regulation, by having a binding mechanism requiring corporate responsibility, we want the same for all sectors of activity. we want the products that are consumed in europe to be free from child labour, free from forced labour and free from deforestation.

on behalf of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the pursuit of profit is a legitimate aim for business, but it must not be achieved at the cost of deliberate violations of human rights or environmental destruction. it therefore seems appropriate to subject companies, and in particular multinationals, to a duty of reasonable vigilance to avoid such abuses. it is a matter of moralizing capitalism. this duty of due diligence has already been imposed in france by a law of 27 march 2017 and i am pleased that this french example is now being followed by the european parliament. however, we must ensure that the future european rule remains reasonable and does not place undue constraints on our companies. it is therefore only possible to support such a text if three conditions are met. first, the duty of vigilance should only apply to subcontractors and partners with whom the company has direct contact and not to the whole supply chain. secondly, it must be clearly seen as an obligation to provide resources and in no case an obligation to achieve results. thirdly, it should only be a burden for large companies, as smes do not have the means to exercise it. i would remind you that this is the position adopted by the french law of 2017 which excludes from its scope companies with less than 5 000 employees.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, mars, nestlé, barry callebaut, mondelēz and other multinational chocolate companies are facing in the united states a lawsuit in which eight children, now young adults, claim that they were used as slave labour on cocoa plantations in the cote d'ivoire. they call for justice, but, the same companies are in the forefront, calling for mandatory human rights and environmental due diligence legislation in the eu. is this a paradox? no. these companies know that years of using voluntary certificates and labels do not allow them to tell that there is no child labour or no deforestation in the chocolate we in europe consume. due diligence legislation does not shift the responsibility from states to companies. states have an obligation to protect human rights and fundamental freedoms – business enterprises are required to respect human rights. the companies must acquire the necessary capacity to do their due diligence in the manner which is reasonable to expect from them. by consulting key stakeholders in their business environment, companies become aware of the most important human rights and environment risks in their entire value chain. due diligence legislation is needed so that there can be a level playing field between companies. as long as we turn a blind eye on human rights violations in corporate value chains, we actually reward those who escape their responsibilities.

member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, our economy is expected to have ended 2020 and started the new year on a weak footing. this morning eurostat confirmed that the euro area experienced negative growth of 0.7% in the last quarter of 2020. but we still expect growth to resume this spring and gather momentum in the summer. so there is hope on the horizon, the famous light ahead of the famous tunnel. but for now, the pandemic continues to hurt people’s livelihoods and the wider economy. and in this context, our first thoughts should be with the victims of the virus, their families and loved ones. in my own country yesterday a very terrible milestone was reached with 100 000 victims. the negative impact on the economy, and we will have to be aware and even proud of this, would have been much bigger without fast and decisive eu and national action. our decision last march to activate the general escape clause was a recognition of the gravity of the unfolding crisis. together with the decisions to adopt a temporary framework for state aid rules and together with the decisions taken by the ecb, these institutions’ reactions enabled member states to deliver an unprecedented fiscal policy response. total direct fiscal support amounted to around 8% of gdp in 2020 in the eu. together with additional liquidity support measures, it is estimated the contraction of gdp in 2020 was cushioned by around 4.5 percentage points. national and common fiscal response go hand-in-hand. i think we should consider them together. when we, for example, make a comparison with what is happening in america, in the us, we should consider the two parallel track of the european reaction. we are not a federal state. we have to combine and make national and common reactions go together. i am telling this because, today, probably the american, the us house, will finally approve the 1.9 trillion stimulus package of the new us administration. this will also have a very positive impact in our economy. but all in all, i think we have to consider the whole reaction and not only our common tools. vital liquidity support made possible by the temporary framework for state aid prevented liquidity shortages from turning into solvency problems. and we would have had solvency problems without this extraordinary authorised state aid. and we had the contrary. we had the lowest level of bankruptcies in many years in 2020. now our message is clear to further cushion the impact of the crisis. fiscal support should continue as long as needed. and it is the same message which comes from multilateral fora such as the g7 and the g20 and by key international partners. we must ensure that we don’t repeat the mistakes of a decade ago by pulling back support too soon. and so for 2022, it is clear that fiscal support will still be necessary. better to err towards doing too much rather than too little. based on current indications, the general escape clause would remain active in 2022 and be deactivated in 2023. i think conditions are there for a strong recovery. again today, the oecd confirmed in its forecast a growth for 2021 of 3.9% and for 2022 of 3.8%, which is very near to the commission’s forecasts presented one month ago. this is a strong economic growth for an economic area such as the eu. so we will face the crisis. we are still five points behind what we were at the end of 2019, but we have all conditions for a strong recovery in the next couple of years, beginning this summer. fiscal policy then should be differentiated according to the pace of each country’s recovery and the underlying fiscal situation. timely, temporary and targeted measures will allow us to move towards a return to sustainable budgets in the medium-term, ensuring debt sustainability while also enhancing investment. finally, a few remarks on our common eu tools. of course, the rrf is a game changer. its effective implementation could deliver up to 2% of additional gdp by 2024 and create two million jobs, including by accelerating the green and digital transition. it is vital that we make use of this opportunity to increase both the level and the quality of our investment. investeu is another critical tool. we discussed it this morning, and i think that also thanks to the strong support of parliament, this tool will also help to crowd private investment for the transition. the sure instrument has proven very popular. we have already proposed over 90 billion of support out of a total envelope of 100 billion and 19 member states asked for this support despite the very low interest rate environment we are in. finally, ease, effective active support to employment, is also a new and important tool. it provides guidance for member states to accompany labour market transitions from decline into expanding sectors, including green and digital, anticipating change and assisting workers affected by restructuring processes. so, ladies and gentlemen, honourable members, with the economic outlook surrounded by uncertainty, we must deliver on all these fronts to make sure the recovery takes hold on our economies, that we emerge renewed and more resilient from the crisis and stronger for the twin transition. this should equip our economy for the present and for the future. and fairer because no one should be left behind.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the pandemic has plunged the economy into a deep recession. the economy has collapsed and public debts have exploded in many member states. if we want to get out of this situation, we need two things: vaccines, vaccines, vaccines and economic growth. but to generate this growth, it is not enough to simply turn the money faucet up even more. finally, we have already seen before the pandemic that the engine of growth in many european countries has faltered considerably. the aim must not be to return to the growth path we had before the crisis, but to find a new, steeper path and take it. to do this, we need two things: structural reforms and investments that significantly increase the potential for growth, including, of course, in the areas of sustainability and digitization. with the facility for building and resilience, which we adopted at the last meeting, with investeu, which we discussed this morning - as the commissioner pointed out - and with a new fund at the european investment bank, we have created programmes at european level which should enable member states to make these investments. now, two things matter most. firstly, the money must flow quickly. in the case of eib funds, it was the member states themselves who caused the failure to flow quickly, and this must not happen again. the commission has also been asked to take a number of measures to ensure that the member states do not use the funds they have earmarked for the right investments and thus stimulate longterm growth.

madam president, documents leaked from the german government have clearly shown that the fear of the corona was deliberately generated, and the same is true of european debt. it's time to put people first. the people of europe are in debt slavery, and the coronavirus crisis has only deepened that debt slavery. the monetary system that we live in, that we have in europe, is based on creating money out of nothing, ex nihilo, or printing or electronically, so at the touch of a button on a computer, often without a cover. the only way for citizens to get that money is to take out loans, expensive and long-term. it's time to break the shackles of debt slavery. it's time for central banks to pay, therefore, a certain monthly amount directly into the account of the citizens, debt-free, free, positive, non-credit money. we see this as one way to fund the unconditional, universal basic income program. wealth can only come from freedom, ladies and gentlemen. only by being free from fear, stress and debt can our fellow citizens realize their full life potential. only then can we talk about real development.

author. madam president, since 2011, following the peaceful uprising of the bahraini people, the bahraini authorities have carried out a severe crackdown on the rights and freedoms of the population, in particular the rights to peaceful demonstration, freedom of expression, digital freedom. as an example, we can mention that in february 2021, a month ago, the arrest of many children who had joined demonstrations commemorating the tenth anniversary of the so-called arab spring uprising, were subject to threats of rape, electrocution. three of them are still in prison today. in addition, death sentences and executions are taking place at an alarming rate, often following confessions obtained under torture and after trials deemed unfair. other factors include the dissolution by the bahraini authorities of the largest opposition party, al-wefaq, which is a peaceful opposition, the persecution of public figures for their activities on social media, and the deplorable conditions in bahrain's overcrowded prisons. it is therefore our duty to continue our work to encourage the country to take measures that go in the direction of respect for human rights and in this regard, i am addressing you, mr high representative, mr borrell, bahrain and other countries use the fact of having a dialogue on human rights as proof that the government respects human rights. we know that this is not the case and the european union must be particularly careful not to be trapped by this kind of talk, because we need action. the speech, yes, the dialogue, yes, but when the speech is not followed by positive actions, it becomes blabber, it becomes useless and unproductive. i hope that there will be a dialogue again, followed by positive developments in the interest of human rights in bahrain.
author. – madam president, in the aftermath of the bahraini arab spring uprising, bahrain authorities continued to violate and restrict fundamental freedoms. human rights lawyers, journalists and political activists face ongoing systematic targeting, and no political opposition is tolerated. the situation of human rights defenders, and women particularly, is extremely worrying. bahrain has continued to execute people, despite the 2017 moratorium on the death penalty, and 27 persons are currently on death row, being subject to extreme psychological suffering and inhumane conditions in prisons. in our resolution, we strongly call on the bahraini authorities to halt their execution immediately and to commute their sentences; this includes releasing all human rights defenders and prisoners of conscience and dropping all charges against them. the eu and its member states should continue to put pressure on the authorities to ensure respect for human rights as part of any enhanced eu cooperation with the country.
