the next item is the debate on the request for an oral answer to the commission on the long-term vision for rural areas by mr norbert lins, on behalf of the committee on agriculture and rural development (o-000005/2021 b9-0006/21) (2021/2549 ((rsp)).
a writer. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i have the honour to present our oral question on the long-term vision for rural areas in europe. i am not speaking here merely as chairman of the committee on agriculture, but as someone who grew up in the countryside and knows the opportunities, the benefits, but also the challenges of rural areas. i therefore look forward with great anticipation to the commission's communication on a long-term vision for the future of rural europe up to 2040. the aim is to stimulate a debate at european level on the future of rural areas and their place in society. a place in society and the acceptance of rural areas are crucial for me. rural areas provide not only food, recreation and the preservation of traditions, but also a way of living together, which is increasingly being lost in today's individualistic society. and they are also, above all, economic spaces, cultural spaces, they need their own infrastructure and they have their own lives, they are not just annexes of urban spaces where you can relax on the weekend. for me, rural areas are the heart of europe and what europe is at its core. they represent the diversity and the diversity of our countries and regions. but rural areas also have to keep pace with climate change, social and economic challenges. the commission must address the urgent issues for rural areas. it is about transport planning and mobility, about school and education, about demography and welfare, broadband and social cohesion and civic engagement. coordination between the different policies involved will play a central role in ensuring the success of the rural agenda. rural development under the common agricultural policy will be crucial to support rural areas. regional and local authorities also play a crucial role in the social change needed to achieve the objectives set by the european union. they are at the forefront of tackling the challenges facing europe. for example, ladies and gentlemen, one positive aspect is the issue of climate. i am convinced that rural areas will be the first in the european union to achieve climate neutrality, because they not only emit emissions, but are able to capture carbon through growing forests, grasslands and arable land. i therefore ask the commission specifically how it intends to achieve the following: firstly, the coherence between the cap and the other policies. secondly, the involvement of regional and local authorities in rural areas in the preparation, analysis, design and implementation of the long-term vision for rural areas. thirdly, the alignment of the strategic plans with the future vision for rural areas, including, for example, the question of whether there is a mid-term review. fourthly, the appropriate and treaty-compliant distinction between the financing of rural areas under the cap and cohesion policy, taking into account the objectives of each policy area as described in articles 39 and 174 tfeu. i am very much looking forward to your answer, commissioner. thank you very much.
member of the commission. – mr president, i am very grateful for the opportunity to discuss the future of rural areas with you. article 174 of the treaty stipulates that particular attention should be paid to rural areas in reducing regional disparities and addressing backwardness. i am very thankful to mr lins for mentioning it. unfortunately, in the current regional development fund regulation, adopted in 2013, the word ‘urban’ was mentioned 46 times, while the word ‘rural’ was only mentioned five times, and always in relation with urban. fortunately, we can see some progress in the recently-agreed common provisions regulation, where a reference to rural and local has been introduced. there is a growing perception that the role and importance of rural areas is underappreciated: 56 percent of the respondents in remote rural areas felt left behind by society. this needs to be addressed in light of our overall eu objectives for rural areas. the european union needs to get closer to its rural citizens. this is precisely the purpose of the long-term vision, which i will be developing together with my colleague, commissioner ferreira, and under the supervision of vice-president šuica. the vision is intended to provide an ambitious, yet realistic, idea of how europe wants its rural areas to look by 2040. about the question of how the commission will ensure coherence between the cap and other policies involved in developing our long-term vision for rural areas, as announced in our communication on the vision for mid-2021, we will translate our goals into a number of actions that can enhance the synergies of our policies and of our funding instruments to support rural areas. we have identified the concept of rural proofing, drawing on territorial impact assessments and better monitoring of rural areas as key actions for the future. europe has made full use of its policies and funds in supporting the development of rural areas in a coherent manner to make sure that all of them pull in the same direction. last june, i, together with vice-president šuica and commissioner ferreira, sent out a letter to member states asking them to fully and coherently use the possibilities under all policies supporting the development of rural areas and to ensure that the specific needs of rural areas are duly taken into account, both under the cohesion policy programmes and under the cap strategic plans. in december, as a part of our cap recommendations to member states, we stated that addressing the structural challenges and inducing favourable dynamics across rural areas will require mobilising a mix of public and private resources and initiatives, using the cap as well as other european policies and funds. more recently, the commission provided member states with guidelines to support them in the preparation of the recovery and resilience plans to assist member states in preparing their plans. our main target should be social and territorial cohesion. as mentioned in the plan, we have been very clear that member states should seek compatibility and possible synergy in their recovery plans. as a part of our guidelines, we ask that member states should outline how their plans will contribute to enhancing cohesion, taking into account local, regional and national disparities, including their rural—urban gaps. in particular, we stated to member states that investing in rural areas through a long—term vision will stimulate urgently-needed economic growth in those areas and contribute to reducing the digital divide. i’m happy to inform you that, from a preliminary assessment of some of the draft plans, it seems most member states will follow the commission’s advice. member states are at different stages of preparation, but we already see some good examples of planned investments in the agricultural bioeconomy, agri—food chains and forestry. several instruments also foreseen for rural areas are broadband, health and local communities as well as rural transport accessibility. about the question of how the cap, which is currently being negotiated, and particularly strategic plans, will be harmonised with the future vision for rural areas, the cap strategic plans will help us to address issues such as generational renewal in agriculture and rural business development, which are key for the future of rural areas. it is important to underline once again that the cap alone cannot address the challenges faced by rural areas, and the vision goes beyond agriculture. and the last question about how the commission intends to draw a proper and treaty—compatible distinction between funding for rural areas under cap and under cohesion policy, taking into account the objectives of articles 39 and 174 of the treaty: rather than a focus on distinction and delimitation between funding under the cap and cohesion policy, the commission is addressing how to improve synergies and complementarities between funds to better address the specific and very diverse needs of rural areas. i would like to give you one example on complementarity. farmers who supply the short supply chain should be supported through the cap, whereas the processing industry should be supported through the recovery fund. member states should also consider using the recovery and resilience fund to accelerate reforms and address the challenges faced by rural areas, including those posed by demographic change, and making them better prepared for the challenges and opportunities of the green and digital transitions.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the more people live in cities, the less attention is paid to the countryside. and in this european union, three quarters of the people live in cities, and accordingly the role of the countryside is often forgotten. it creates jobs, it creates landscapes, the people in the countryside feed us because the farmers are there, but the countryside also creates security. i therefore believe that the rural areas deserve attention and that people living in rural areas have the same right to a quality of life as people in cities. i think it is right that the commission has announced that it is going to proceed with a fundamental reflection, a strategy for rural areas, and we are having a debate on this. people in rural areas have the right to school, they have the right to health care, they have the right to the internet and much more. and above all, rural areas are not simply appendages to cities, and rural areas cannot absorb what has gone wrong in cities. the fact that concrete is being used, that biodiversity is being destroyed, does not mean that rural areas have to do all this. rural areas are not just nature reserves, they are living spaces where people have to live. rural areas also have a right to be led, including politically, by those who live in rural areas. i think that local governance is also extremely important.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the negotiations, but that it should be involved in the negotiations. but the concrete and harmonious development of the rural world requires, above all, very clear coordination and coherence between the different policies and decision-making levels. i therefore stress the importance of action by the actors and authorities at regional and local level who, on the ground, in rural areas, are the real experts in their realities. we therefore hope that the commission will correct its position and unambiguously defend the partnership principle of the new cap. we have the support of the commission for parliament's position in the cap negotiations in defending the fundamental role of the regions and regional and local authorities and in ensuring their current role in rural development.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, the villages in my country, romania, are emptying. of the nearly five million romanians who have gone to work outside the country, the vast majority come from rural areas. villages in romania and many other european countries are emptying because they no longer offer opportunities, especially for young people, because they often lack access to basic infrastructure, education or health. when we think about the future of rural areas, i don't want to think about a future where villages become museum pieces. i want us to think about how we harness the natural, human and cultural resources that exist in the villages to bring young people back. the good news is that we have the tools at european level to do this, but we need an integrated strategy. first, we need to invest in the development of basic infrastructure, we need to build hospitals, schools and roads in the villages. secondly, we need to make the cap work for real farmers, to encourage young farmers, to protect biodiversity. and thirdly, we must make villages attractive for non-agricultural activities too, whether it be tourism, cultural activities, the relocation of small entrepreneurs from towns to villages, all these are things we must support including at european level and i hope that the future strategy you are preparing, commissioner, will go in this direction.

mr. chairman, thank you. ladies and gentlemen, when we talk about the vision for rural areas, it is impossible, apart from agriculture, to mention energy. so, collecting and consuming has defined how and where we live throughout history as well as social relations throughout history. the density of the energy source is a key factor in this. for one gigawatts of power to come from, say, solar power, it takes a lot more space than for one gigawatts to come from, say, nuclear power. for much of the past, energy was based on wood, on biomass, and 90 percent of people lived in rural areas. then we have industrialization in the middle of the 18th century, and we've got to the point where today three-quarters of europeans live in cities. now we go back to low-density energy sources, which require a lot of space. and where is there enough space for solar power, for wind power? in the countryside. where's the biomass? in the countryside. however, none of this will happen unless the money actually stays in those spaces. we in croatia have devastated rural areas by centralizing the money from the forests, which they are rich in. so he was leaving those premises. that can't happen with energy. the energy money must stay in rural areas, which are its main source of future.
rapporteur. – mr president, more than a year has passed since europe, together with the rest of the world, was struck by one of the worst crisis events in a generation. the covid-19 pandemic has affected all aspects of our lives. many have lost their loved ones, or have not been able to visit their families. millions of bars, restaurants and businesses had to shut down. theatres, museums and classrooms continue to stay empty. europeans have struggled greatly in this period and our hardships, unfortunately, are still not over. despite the fact that the months ahead of us are unpredictable, we cannot stay idle. we have the responsibility to put forward a vision and adapt to newer circumstances. with this in mind, i have worked on the guidelines for the next annual budget of the european union. the programmes, policies and agencies we have built over the years have been facing tremendous new challenges. this is why the next annual eu budget has to be a recovery budget in all its aspects. a recovery fit for all regions, all sectors and all generations. first of all, we need to ensure that small and medium-sized enterprises receive our full support. smes constitute the backbone of the european economy, representing almost 99% of all businesses in europe and providing around three-quarters of all jobs. only by protecting that backbone that has been hit particularly hard can we ensure a vibrant economy that boosts investments and tackles unemployment. furthermore, the next budget should provide all the tools needed to close the digital divide and enable europe’s economy and public sector to be at the forefront of the accelerated digital transition. this transition obviously also needs to be in line with the targets set in the european green deal, by turning green challenges into investments and opportunities in order to achieve a more sustainable, inclusive and resilient economy. the covid-19 crisis has put european health systems under unprecedented stress and exposed shortcomings in production capacities for vaccinations and other essential medical products in the union. so in order to achieve our budgetary priorities, we will need to ensure a strong european health union based on solidarity and collective responsibility. – kolegice i kolege, pandemija je još jednom pokazala ogromne zdravstvene i gospodarske razlike između država članica. zato su ključne kohezijske politike koje bi novijim državama članicama trebale omogućiti hvatanje koraka u razvoju, ne zato da bi se same države što bolje pozicionirale nego radi toga kako bi svim europskim građanima osigurali jednake uvjete zdravstvene zaštite i jednake mogućnosti. u tom kontekstu, ne možemo ignorirati niti činjenicu da se cijela europa, a osobito neki njezini dijelovi suočavaju s negativnim demografskim trendovima. demografski deficit dugoročni je problem koji zaslužuje sveobuhvatan pristup. stoga je nužno osvijestiti važnost demografske komponente svih europskih politika pa i u kontekstu članka 174. ugovora o funkcioniranju europske unije. poseban fokus u ovim smjernicama stavili smo na mladu generaciju. mladi su još jednom posebno pogođeni posljedicama ove krize, uz member of the commission, on behalf of the vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – mr president, honourable members, the eu has a long—standing and clear position on the freedom of religion or belief. it remains a key priority of human rights in line with the eu action plan on human rights and democracy 2020—2024. pakistan’s blasphemy laws carry very harsh sentences, including the death penalty, which the eu strongly opposes under any circumstances. they are often misused to silence opponents and their defenders, and to discriminate against religious minorities with severe negative effects on the rights of freedom of expression and freedom of religion or belief. ahmadi muslims are probably the most at risk. the hr / vp, josep borrell, reiterated the eu’s concerns regarding the application of the death penalty and the misuse of blasphemy legislation during the last eu—pakistan strategic dialogue with foreign minister qureshi. the tragic cases of individuals on death row because of blasphemy accusations also point to the structural weaknesses of the pakistani judicial system and the unequal access to justice. in pakistan, the eu is strongly engaged in promoting and protecting human rights. we are providing support to pakistan to strengthen the rule of law and access to justice and to legal representation. the generalised scheme of preferences plus, the gsp+, granted to pakistan, requires to demonstrate progress on the implementation of 27 international core conventions, and it constitutes a strong leverage for the eu in terms of monitoring any direct advocacy with pakistan. further, through the eu delegation in islamabad and the eu special representative for human rights, the eu regularly raises individual human rights cases with the pakistani authorities at the highest levels. this includes the individual cases of shafqat emmanuel and shagufta kausar. we do this in close contact with civil society partners. honourable members, interfaith dialogue is one of the priority areas of the eu—pakistan strategic engagement plan. in december 2020, the eu delegation in islamabad convened a roundtable to promote interfaith and intrafaith dialogue and understanding. you can rest assured that the eu will continue our regular dialogue on human rights with the government of pakistan and that the particular issues of blasphemy laws and the death penalty remain important points on our agenda.
in writing. the court case involving shagufta kausar and shafqat emmanuel is, along with that of asia bibi, one of the landmark cases of the criminalization of blasphemy in pakistan, since it resulted in the death sentence of these two christians. indeed, while muslims constitute the majority of those charged with blasphemy, as the country's human rights commission notes, religious minorities are disproportionately represented among them. according to data provided by the national commission for peace and justice and quoted by the bbc, 776 muslims, 505 ahmadiyya muslims, 229 christians and 30 hindus were charged under various clauses of the blasphemy law between 1987 and 2018. other yet, non-muslim minorities make up only 3.6% of the country's population. moreover, pakistan seems to be sinking deeper into obscurantism every day. the prime minister's call for the islamic world to emulate this legislation is evidence of this, and is in addition to a litany of outrageous statements, particularly against france. in response, our parliamentary debates will remain in vain as long as the european union persists in extending its generosity to a state that openly flouts our values.

the european parliament will give its green light to the digital europe programme today. as a citizen of the european union and as a reporter on the programme, i am very happy about that. first of all, i want to thank you all again from the bottom of my heart for your great help and support. we made it on time and without delay. today, digital europe is getting its formal end, or rather its grand operational beginning, which frankly we are all looking forward to. from virtual meetings and remote voting, to paperless offices, to supercomputers and artificial intelligence, to smart homes and driverless cars. the digital revolution is happening, in fact, fast and irreversible. at the same time, the negative economic consequences of covid-19 will be severe and severe, and it is here that the digital industry plays a key role as a key tool to increase europe's competitiveness and resilience. as a powerful tool for a better standard of living and a more comfortable daily life and work for citizens, as a driver of significant new revenue streams based on state-of-the-art business models and services. it's digital as well as green. the main objective of this mandate. the main objective of this decade in our modern europe, and the wide application of digital technologies and solutions, is key to overcoming the investment gap affecting both the private and the public sectors. in 2021, as we nostalgically look back on 2019, the importance of digital is undeniable, even for the most skeptical. yes, we've all, whether we like it or not, gone digital in the past year. economies, that digitization must be backed up by true digital transformation. this means a deeper shift that addresses our internal processes, our human skills and our culture, because the birth of europe is reserved for those who underestimate the importance of digital transformation. the digital europe initiative aims to contribute to a stronger europe in which digital technologies also make better healthcare and public services in general possible. better education, better climate change, digital transformation of public and private businesses, transport, tourism, industry, medicine, the whole economy and the whole industry. the commission will continue to support the development of the digital economy, in particular through the development of the digital economy in the union. the digital europe programme aims to contribute to achieving european digital independence and enhancing competitiveness in the global digital economy, while also taking a responsible stance towards new generations, new processes and new jobs. the digital europe programme will play a significant and powerful role in the coming years, but i want to remain absolutely realistic in my forecasts. 7.5 billion euros is a lot of money. and with that money, for example, one country, croatia, can significantly accelerate its transition to e-society. however, digital europe is only one and in itself is still a limited aid, a programme, and it is not a programme to build digital capacities with the most european money. it is therefore important that there is cooperation and synergy between european programmes, that there is coordination, that we know who is working and how. the commission shall be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 264 concerning the establishment of a european data protection supervisor. digital and green, ladies and gentlemen, are therefore the two indispensable elements of every next european programme and project, and the key priorities of the programming period ahead. if europe wants to be a leader in the years ahead, not a follower, a green digital europe really has no alternative. take the field of health care and vaccine development. the challenges that we've faced in the last year because of the coronavirus pandemic are absolutely unprecedented. in such difficult times, we need a europe that is ready and stronger than ever. a europe that has the most advanced technologies, a europe that has the best experts, a europe that can stand up to the virus on its own, a europe that is not lagging behind the world's technological superpowers but is steadily catching up and overtaking them, a europe that is developing its own vaccines and a europe that is investing huge sums in research. that's what we need. digital europe is tomorrow. very soon we will see whether europe will tap into a digital twilight or return to the global technology race's favourite. as a reporter, as a european, i have confidence in this other one.

mr president, commissioner, erasmus+ is without doubt the european project which has contributed most in its more than 30 years of existence to the creation of a genuine european cultural spirit and a european identity, beyond the mere sum of our 27 identities. after months of work, we have today approved the new erasmus+ programme for the period 2021-2027, which will be the most ambitious and inclusive programme to date. i want to acknowledge and congratulate the work of parliament, which has pushed for an increase in the budget and for it to remain a flagship programme within community policies, even though we would have liked to reach the 41 billion euro we proposed. progressives can be satisfied. we have succeeded in turning erasmus+ away from its traditional elitism and into a programme for those who could not afford it. students in higher education, vocational training, adult education and non-formal learning may participate in the programme, which will also be open to people with fewer opportunities, people with disabilities and immigrants. new programmes are being introduced: european universities and centres of vocational excellence, and also discovereu, which gives young people the opportunity to discover europe's diversity while travelling, to learn about europe's heritage and to connect with others. erasmus should now continue to expand into primary and secondary education, creating links with the future european education area to continue to grow and supporting member states in achieving inclusive and quality education. in short, erasmus+ has brought more to the european project than many initiatives and we must continue to invest in erasmus, more than ever, especially in these times when student mobility has been reduced.

mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, artificial intelligence is increasingly being used in the european way of life. 61% of europeans have a positive view of it. yet, 88 percent say that it should be handled with care. as we discuss this important topic in croatia, not far from zagreb, construction is beginning on one of the most advanced technology campuses, which will develop, among other things, autonomous vehicle technology based on artificial intelligence. such examples show that the application of artificial intelligence is the future of the european economy and that it will become a commonplace, an integral part of technologically advanced production in the coming decades. the commission should also be able to provide the necessary information to the member states and the commission on the implementation of the rules on artificial intelligence. and let me also specifically point out the potential that artificial intelligence has in the healthcare field. as many as half of our citizens believe that the health sector is where ai can realize its greatest potential. and that this is indeed the case is evidenced by recent research showing that ai algorithms identify breast cancer with approximately 90 percent accuracy when combined with radiology analysis. in the circumstances in which we have made the fight against cancer our priority, i call on the european institutions to continue to strongly support the development of artificial intelligence, as it provides the possibility of early diagnosis of malignant diseases and thus gives hope to patients for their successful cure.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, our society is facing many environmental challenges, such as the depletion of natural resources and climate change. the negative consequences of uncontrolled business in the form of environmental incidents are increasingly calling for more effective control of business in the private sector. in seeking to reduce its environmental impact, each company should pursue a combined approach that takes into account the direct and indirect economic, social, health and environmental consequences of its decisions and activities. however, it should be noted that the companies cannot be held fully responsible. the commission has also identified a number of areas where the environmental impact of the use of energy is likely to be significant. this prevents a level playing field in the internal market. the commission considers that the aid is compatible with the internal market if it is granted through state resources. the commission should be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 290 tfeu concerning the conditions for the application of this regulation. we need to be focused on preventing harm by strengthening the precautionary principle and the polluter pays principle, creating a level playing field for businesses and ensuring that taxpayers do not bear the cost to the environment. in this respect, the main objective should be to achieve a balance between environmental and business interests. by administrative measures and fines, we can encourage companies to act with due care and their overall corporate social responsibility, and in this way leave a cleaner environment and a safer planet for future generations.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we can rightly say that the european union is the world leader in the protection of personal data. the general data protection regulation guarantees our citizens the security of their data when it is processed. the regulation also provides for the possibility of data transfers with third countries on the basis of an adequacy decision, which is the subject of today's debate. in order to ensure the security of the exchange of personal data, the united kingdom should be able to request the commission to adopt a decision on adequacy in accordance with article 5 of regulation (eu) no 909/2014. this decision-making process does not involve the european parliament, but that does not mean that we as elected representatives of the citizens should be excluded from the whole process. i therefore welcome today's debate and ask that the european commission take this decision and use all available mechanisms to ensure that the personal data of our citizens are also treated in the united kingdom in accordance with the highest standards of legal protection. the united kingdom's legacy of membership is its experience in applying european rules on the protection of personal data. however, i strongly urge the european commission not to hesitate to revoke or suspend an adequacy decision if it is shown that the united kingdom no longer ensures an adequate level of protection of the personal data of our citizens. there must be no exceptions or special omissions. we in the european parliament will continue to monitor on an ongoing basis the trends in the protection of personal data in third countries, including the united kingdom.
mr. chairman, croatian farmers often have problems with the cold. however, the much bigger problem is the interest groups that have been shaping croatian agricultural policy for decades. the aim of these interest groups is to strengthen a few of the largest producers, while leaving little or nothing to a large number of small producers, i.e. feudalization. in croatia, the karst goes all the way from samobor to konoval, and this is an area larger than slavonia and represents a great wealth for the development of agriculture, and that is extensive and ecological which is the future. agroforestry is a systematic land use and management in which trees or shrubs are grown around or between crops or pastures, i.e. it is the combination of trees with crops and pastures. and while in other countries of europe with karst and karst pastures, the moss are in the mediterranean, it is normal for cattle to graze in forests, among trees or in bushes, in croatia this way of production is suppressed. same union, different rules. the same common agricultural policy, different practices. others can, croats cannot. in addition to the decent policy of the government, the responsibility lies with croatian forests, a state-owned company that manages land and forest land and obstructs croatian farmers. we need a change of policy in this sector, and fast.
mr. chairman, the true state of entrepreneurship and entrepreneurial freedoms in croatia is unfortunately best seen in the wood processing sector. when you have a plain in your space, you live off that fertile plain. when you have the sea, you live off the sea, but when you have the forest in space, they don't let you live off the forest. the timber industry has been taken over by a party-cracy that operates on the principle of feeding the likes and the seemingly unlike, or all the rest. never before has a forest been cut down in croatia, especially illegally, and never before has it been harder to get the raw materials. instead of developing entrepreneurship, processing and design, the goal is to export bare raw materials. you can have a contract to buy the raw materials as an entrepreneur, but that doesn't mean you're actually going to get it. you can sue, but by the time the trial is over, you'll be gone because without wood, you simply can't do business. this scandalous policy destroys competition, stifles entrepreneurial freedom and, most importantly, instead of sustainable forest management, we have the devastation of life, the devastation of rural space, the devastation of plant, animal, economic and ultimately human life.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, we have worked very hard on this dossier. once again this year we have identified a number of points which we believe distort competition. every year we do the same job. i wonder how many of the points we have identified in the reports of the past few years have been implemented, have been evaluated by the commission. the commission, before we embark on this task, should not give us an account of how it has used the work of the previous year? i did a study and i saw that, for example, in the 2017 study, the commission analyzed only a few, two or three out of 181 that we had identified. and i think the first point of distortion of competition, commissioner, is the difference in taxation between member states, the use that some member states make of tax policies to take advantage of them. i left from naples, my hometown in southern italy, and stopped to make diesel and paid 1.7 euros for it. i then stopped in luxembourg and paid eur 1.1 for diesel, because luxembourg uses tax incentives to attract investment, to attract consumers, and of course the same thing happens in other countries of the union. this is the real anomaly, the real distortion of competition, together with the placid acceptance of the boycott. we have tried on several occasions to include in this report the absolute condemnation of the boycott of products from a geographical area simply because they come from that geographical area. we have not succeeded because of the obstructionism of the leftist forces among the shadow speakers. yet this is another factor which, for example in italy, has distorted competition, producing irreversible damage to firms which have been discriminated against simply because they come from a geographical area. we shall therefore be working on the same report next year, and we expect a sharp reversal on your part, commissioner, because you are acting and analysing and assessing what we, the sovereign parliament, have identified as a priority.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, dear commissioner, dear colleagues, let me start by thanking the rapporteur, johan van overtveldt, and the shadow rapporteurs, for the excellent cooperation we have had. competition policies are one of the main competencies of the european union, and as a socialist i am very glad that today we are debating a report that takes into account that competition rules are not only about regulating to ensure fair conditions, but also about the role they play in the achievement of the digital and the environmental transitions. competition is not only about low prices, it’s about quality and innovation of products and of production chains. these are crucial elements if we want to reach the goals of the green deal. and we have to reach these goals, because they are not just a ‘nice to have’, they are a must! therefore, i want to underline once more how important it is not only to foster innovation through competition but also for member states not to distort this phenomenon. even each justified state aid must be in line with the green deal, with the sustainable development goals and our pillar of social rights. this report also takes into account that loopholes in the existing legislation exist when it comes to the digital markets. while we are all aware that data is a source of considerable economic power and leverage, often procedures – especially in the antitrust field – are way too slow to keep up with fast—developing business models in the digital markets and also because ex ante interventions are so far forbidden. meanwhile, the european union is watching gatekeepers become ever more powerful – the recent data scandals show us the necessity to act: for example, the facebook—whatsapp merger, where facebook lied about its technical abilities on using data from whatsapp for personalised adds on facebook. in fact, one could also call the unwillingness of the ceos of the gafas (google, amazon, facebook, apple) to appear in front of this parliament as the next scandal. the upcoming dma (digital market act) is thus a very good opportunity, which must be used to complement competition policies. nevertheless, there is also room for improvement when it comes to antitrust and merger procedures, and lastly also to the sanction mechanisms – a conclusion that has already been drawn many times before in this annual report, and where the commission finally must act. allow me at the end to underline that the european commission needs to continue actively monitoring and removing unjustified geo—blocking, and as an mep from luxembourg, a small member state, i also call on the commission in view of a fully functioning single market to address the anti—competitive effects of the so—called territorial supply constraints.
for the greens/efa group madam president, dear commissioner vestager, dear colleagues, 2020 will not be a year of crisis for everyone. for some companies, it is even a memorable and terrible year: 2020 is the year of records in terms of state aid. in france, 100% of large enterprises have received exceptional public aid, without any ecological or social conditions. as our smes and small businesses struggle to survive, 26 of our 40 national champions pay €51 billion in dividends to their shareholders, 22% more than last year: the french budget equivalent of national education or the staff costs of public hospitals. for bruno le maire, the french finance minister, it is in times of crisis that we should take advantage of the state's money. in saying this, he was not addressing the exploding numbers of people seeking food aid, or the desperate students, or the burned-out carers, or the nine million poor people in france. he was talking to the big bosses, and they got the message. crisis or not, the economy, for them, is all about moving wealth from the taxpayer's pocket to the shareholder's. and this without compensation, without leakage, but with concentration of wealth, with layoffs, with tax evasion and opacity, with denial of the climate emergency. ladies and gentlemen, without the conditionality associated with state aid, the world after will be very much like the world before, and worse, and we do not accept that. too many harmful consequences will follow. this is serious.

and/or pass on those digital taxes to users and consumers. the real challenge we face is the monopoly of these companies. after all, a digitax does not change that situation. it smothers smaller, innovative companies – they do not even get the chance to grow, with all the associated consequences in terms of innovation, growth and even employment. this report examines the role of these digital players in more detail, and the relationship with governments and consumers. it welcomes the commission’s determination to address unfair terms and practices of platforms acting as gatekeepers and eliminate illegitimate obstacles to online competition in the european digital single market. however, the slowness of anti-trust investigations mismatches fast-moving digital markets. the report is particularly critical of the commission’s handling of the google search bias case, as well as the takeover of the whatsapp messenger service by facebook. structural unbundling of big tech monopolies may be desirable for restoring competition in digital markets, given the limits of fines and the failure of past behavioural remedies in certain anti-trust cases. the report calls on the commission to review its merger and acquisition rules when it comes to asserting personal data. in summary, we ask the commission to monitor the evolutions in the digital market with persistence, efficiency and the appropriate speed. the report is, of course, not limited to the digital market. at the tail-end of the covid crisis are important challenges on the road to efficient recovery. in that regard, we welcome the adoption of the temporary framework for state aid measures established in response to the unprecedented covid—19 crisis. we call on the commission to be sure and monitor the proper use and distribution of the different eu funding measures in response to the covid-19 crisis, which should be in accordance with eu competition and state aid rules. we are in need of a post covid-19 roadmap for better-targeted state aid in order to promote competitiveness and safeguard jobs. we also urge the commission to pay attention to the role of the foreign-based state-owned enterprises that are supported and subsidised by their governments in ways that the eu single market rules prohibit for eu entities. there has to be some serious concern about distortive state-funded competition from foreign undertakings acquiring european undertakings. last but not least, the report calls for reflection on possible distortions of competition arising from the european central bank’s corporate bonds purchases. we’re at an absolutely historical point for our economies, for the welfare of our citizens. do we succeed in bringing about a sustained and healthy recovery? appropriate monetary and budgetary policies are much needed, but so is also a robust competition policy. let us avoid the historic mistake made by the american president, franklin roosevelt, in his new deal. roosevelt’s national industrial recovery act (nira) of 1933 contained many solid, constructive and much-needed measures, but this nira also limited competition, allowed cartels, allowed collusion and even stimulated price fixing. a lot of research has gone into nira and the results are unequivocal. the results of these anti-competitive measures were a substantial rise in inflation, a reduction of growth by at least 25%, and substantially less job creation. let us avoid these kinds of mistakes. let us take robust competition policy and its implementation very seriously.
thank you very much mr van overtveldt, and my apologies. i was not aware of any problems in connection and i will definitely report the case. thank you very much. we continue with the debate.
madam president, last friday the commissioner announced she has opened a formal antitrust investigation into facebook in cooperation with the uk competition and markets authority, and the claim is that facebook has constructed an unfair advantage on the online advertising sector. this goes under the heading: better late than never. today we will discuss the annual competition policy report looking back at 2020, and over a year ago we adopted a report with similar parts, in which a vast majority of parliament called on the commissioner to propose legislation restricting facebook’s main revenue stream: digital advertising. for years, the market share of facebook and google have increased at an exponential rate, and today we are facing a duopoly. so parliament cannot but repeat itself that there is a significant problem insufficiently tackled. i am glad that the commission has put forward legislation that will address part of the market dominance of some giants, but as shadow rapporteur on the digital market tax i have to express my great concern. market definition is still the same; neither the digital markets act nor the digital services act will change the digital advertising market fundamentally, and interoperability measures for core services are missing. so in short, the motto – if you choose to accept it, commissioner – is: let’s move fast and break monopoly power.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the liberal ideology which inspired the european treaties, competition has always been the sole objective. slaves to the theory that competition will endlessly optimize creativity and performance, you have built a world in which all economic actors are expected to be constantly vigilant and efficient. this race for profitability, which has sacrificed millions of jobs, has been an inhumane and stupid system that has allowed big business, through globalization, through offshoring, to escape the control of their states and to abuse their rules in countries where there are none. you are beginning to take stock of this disaster and are proposing a reform of public aid which will finally promote european research and projects, particularly in the digital field, whereas yesterday you refused this voluntarism on the grounds that it was not fair to non-european countries. our group has always denounced unfair competition and its ravages. the corrective measures introduced by the european union are not enough. it is a real ideological break that must be made, by returning to intelligent protectionism, by introducing national preference in certain public procurement, by reviving growth through investment. this project must be one of reason and social peace, as opposed to the permanent conflict of competition. we've been right for 30 years. it's time to move on and trust each other.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the house. i am pleased and i commend the rapporteur's work in emphasising competitiveness in addition to state aid, even though i have myself experienced this as a "sitochrome" in the left-wing dictionary in many debates. competitiveness is the key to the future if we don't want to fall behind the us and asia. that is why i also agree that the transitional state aid rules should be in place until the european economy recovers. what i regret is that the report presents a left-wing narrative on taxation. while the text recognises that tax policy is a competence of the member states, it targets countries that support tax cuts. let us do everything we can to ensure that no one, not even the most, is able to avoid paying taxes, but let us also recognise that fair tax competition contributes to increasing competitiveness by encouraging companies to remain in the union. i am proud to say that hungary is one of the best examples of how to increase state revenues, achieve economic growth above the eu average and reduce unemployment through tax cuts. it should be understood that internal competitiveness also increases the eu's competitiveness.
madam president, (inaudible) triggered by the covid—19 pandemic has been especially severe for enterprises, mostly smes, in tourism, hospitality and culture, not least those based in eu island regions. determination of major transport connections and interruption of ongoing business relations cannot be brought back to their original base overnight. state aid is crucial, but needs time to work effectively. some would like a so—called return to normal on economic regulation, and for this to happen as soon as possible. but this has no real—life logic to back it, only dogma. the idea that the re—application of state aid rules must follow the same tempo as the epidemiological situation is absurd. the two follow different time clocks. rather, the exceptional measures adopted under the temporary framework for the hospitality and culture sectors as affected by the covid—19 crisis must be maintained, no matter what the received wisdom about competition and what the rules say. yes, the lessons of the 2008 financial crisis must be kept in mind, but they cannot be taken as the be—all—and—end all compass by which to navigate today’s storm. otherwise, we risk the collapse of major sectors of activity, like tourism and culture.

in writing. competition policy is an important tool for strengthening the single market. fair competition also ensures strong consumer protection. in recent years, however, both businesses and consumers have experienced the digital transformation of the economy. these developments have created gaps in traditional competition policy and the antitrust model has been too slow to respond to these changes. i therefore welcome the european commission's focus on modernising our competition policy to meet the challenges of the digital sector. i was the author of the opinion on competition policy in the committee on the internal market, which focused on the role of 'gateway' platforms. in particular, when it comes to e-commerce, for example, the dominance of a few platforms has serious consequences for consumer choice and market access for many european businesses. the european union needs to adapt its competition instruments to recent and future market developments, and to this end, parliament has started negotiations on a digital market legislation setting out the obligations and prohibitions that content controllers must comply with in their daily work.
in writing. the past year has been a challenge in all areas of our lives. the economy is in deep trouble, and our job is to make sure that the recovery is as cost-effective as possible. this report highlights our competitive and development potential for growth. the digital environment is not geographically limited, which means that our understanding of the sector needs to be updated independently, especially with regard to large technology companies. if we want to preserve our independence and the rules of fair competition, we must take account of what is said in this report.
the next item is the brief presentation of the report by ms monika vana on the gender dimension in cohesion policy (2020/2040 (ini)) (a9-0154/2021).
rapporteur. – mr president, i am very glad to have the opportunity to present this report on the gender dimension in cohesion policy, which comes at a very timely moment. i would first like to take this opportunity to thank all the shadow rapporteurs, including you, chair, for the work and the good cooperation on this report. the principle of gender equality is a core value of the european union, applicable to all eu activities and policies. among the eu policy instruments, cohesion policy is an especially impactful tool and, by the way, a large part of the eu budget. at the next plenary, the common provisions regulation (cpr) is expected to be adopted, the overarching regulation for cohesion policy funds. the cpr will already include gender equality as a horizontal principle. this report on the gender dimension in cohesion policy is based on a study for the committee on regional development (regi) on gender equality and cohesion policy for the previous funding period, and includes areas for improvement in the upcoming period. regarding the negotiations on the report, i want to say that, while there was unanimous agreement that the topic is of crucial importance for regi and for the whole european parliament, there were very big differences in approach and aims and how best to achieve them among the groups. but i think that, with this report, we have now managed to strike a balance between the views of the different groups. most importantly, we were able to formulate a strong position of the european parliament and to give concrete recommendations for improvements in this upcoming cohesion fund period. let me mention some of the most important points. the report emphasises the importance of a coordinated governance framework on gender equality, national guidelines and technical support. it highlights the partnership principle and the cooperation between the eu level and the national, local and regional levels. the report stresses that all programmes in cohesion policy should ensure gender equality throughout their preparation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. in this report, we stress the need for a gender equality strategy with clear objectives and targets at national and regional level as ex—ante requirements, and we call for ex—ante and ex—post gender impact assessment. we stress that measurable indicators, where appropriate, should also enable the support of gender equality to be monitored. furthermore, the report highlights gender budgeting and the closing of the gender data gap. as i mentioned, the partnership principle is very important. we mention training and capacity building of managing authorities, guidance documents and the role of the european institute for gender equality. very important in this report is also the call on the commission that member states and their respective authorities should follow the principles of the rule of law, including the principle of non-discrimination. last, but not least, we recognise in this report the burden placed on women as principal caregivers in formal and informal settings, as well as their social value, especially during the covid—19 crisis, and the rise of domestic violence and the need for projects and more fight against domestic and other kinds of gender—based violence. we know, of course, that this report is only a starting point to improve gender equality in cohesion policy, and we still have a lot of work ahead of us. let’s work together on that. we are very happy that the commission is present today. finally, i want to ask the commission, after the final vote of the parliament tomorrow, to take note of the position of the european parliament and to really make gender equality a cornerstone of eu cohesion policy, to go beyond words and to strengthen gender equality in practice.
member of the commission. – mr president, it is an honour to address you today on behalf of my fellow commissioner ferreira. one of the saddest features of crisis – and the current one is no exception – is the aggravation of old inequalities. we see this at this regional level; we see this at the social level; and we see it in terms of gender. on two key front lines in this battle, the home front and the health care front, women have borne the brunt of the battle, because they are the majority of home workers and they are the majority of health care workers. from childcare to layoffs, from health risks to domestic violence, women have struggled and women have suffered. in terms of gender equality, hard fought gains – the work of decades – risk being rolled back. so this report is very timely and i thank the regional development committee (regi) and the rapporteur, ms monika vana, for their excellent report, and the committee on women’s rights and gender equality (femm) for their opinion. as the report notes, cohesion policy plays a crucial role in gender equality. it plays a crucial role in two critical ways. first, by cohesion investments, we invest in better quality childcare and we promote access to that childcare. we invest in support for female entrepreneurs, female researchers and female innovators. we ensure equal access to upskilling and training, and we invest in other services from public transport to it, to health care, education and other social services – essential services, which are particularly crucial for women. zooming out from these targeted actions, these investments will help ensure that women participate equally in the green and digital transitions. secondly, we support gender equality by our process. gender equality is a crosscutting principle for all cohesion policy investments. you are right, gender equality is not a competing objective, but a shared goal. it must be taken into account at every step, from the preparation of programmes to project selection and implementation, to programme monitoring and evaluation. and bodies fighting for fundamental rights, for non-discrimination and for women, are key partners at each step of the process. so by investment and a comprehensive process, we support gender equality. but we agree with the report’s recommendation that more must be done. already, in the new programme starting this year, we are reinforcing requirements. first, it is now a precondition of funding, what we call an enabling condition, that projects respect the eu charter of fundamental rights, including the requirement on gender equality. second, there is now a system for tracking gender-related expenditure across cohesion policy funding, and this will allow for better monitoring of gender equality in our investments. the report also points to a lack of knowledge among stakeholders, and the commission continues to build capacity in this area. we organised technical seminars with managing authorities, and we communicate with stakeholders on concrete actions they can take to promote gender equality. so the commission is providing a comprehensive framework to promote gender equality in cohesion policy. but eu funding alone cannot do everything for gender equality and i call on member states to make the maximum use of every element of this framework. we in the commission will continue to support member states: where a lack of capacity is a bottleneck, technical assistance is at their disposal. we will continue to do everything we can and to consult with this parliament and work together in this crucial area. i assure you of our commitment in programme preparation and beyond. we cannot allow women to be left behind in the recovery. we cannot lose a decade or more of hard-won gains in terms of equality, and we will not if we work together.
the examination of this point is concluded. the vote will take place tomorrow.

when the commission published the eu biodiversity strategy in may last year, it drew on a clear scientific evidence. healthy nature shapes the environment and the climate in which humanity can thrive. long—term budget, in particular under common agricultural policy funds, as well as in the national recovery and resilience plans. the process for preparing the post—2020 global biodiversity framework eu and around the world.

mr. chairman, today, it's going to be hard to find a beach, a forest, a meadow, even a mountain top without a piece of trash. the waste is, unfortunately, found in caves and pits a mile deep and on the bottom of a sea that is increasingly looking like a dump. the worst of all is plastic, which is hard to break down. in the environment, it shrinks to smaller and smaller pieces of microplastics, and then smaller and smaller pieces of nanoplastics, which enter all the pores of life. we're literally drinking and eating plastic and it's contaminating all life on earth. the biggest blow to biodiversity in recent times is certainly the covid crisis waste, and i'm talking about masks first and foremost. we're talking about trillions and trillions, so thousands and thousands of billions of masks that have been used in this crisis. a lot of them will end up in the neighborhood. it's destroying life in the sea, but also on land. scientific research shows that just one mask a day can release several hundred thousand plastic micro-fibers that further contaminate everything around them. these are, unfortunately, the terrible consequences of the wrong policies in the covid crisis based more on the cult of opinion and dogma than on real science and scientific support for research.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the competitiveness of our companies and the protection of consumers depend heavily on the ability of the authorities to react to infringements of intellectual property rights and the marketing of counterfeit goods. moreover, customs fraud drains public funds and, given its transnational nature, requires close cooperation between the administrations of the member states. we therefore support the initiative to allocate financial resources for the purchase and use of new and more advanced customs control equipment. the financial instrument to be made available to member states stems from the increasingly urgent need to significantly strengthen controls on goods as they enter the union's borders in order to ensure their safety. these goods must be safe and comply with the same european union regulations that our companies are required to comply with in order to market their products. the intention to support the achievement of an adequate and uniform level in all the countries of the union is also to be welcomed. this will allow the benefits of the customs union to be realised, providing further support to customs authorities in their joint action to protect the interests of the member states and the single market.

the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner reynders, this june marks 42 years since the first direct democratic elections to the european parliament and in december it will be 12 years since the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon. this means that for forty years this european parliament has had no lack of democratic legitimacy and no shortfall in legislative competence. it is a parliament which has been legislating since the treaty of lisbon and the charter of fundamental rights on competences which were for a long time the competences of the member states, including nothing less than free movement, which is a fundamental right enshrined in the charter, in article 45, and which is the legal basis of this instrument. and yet this parliament, which has no deficit of democratic legitimacy or legislative competence, does have it to be news. he has a serious problem with getting news, and even more, if you will, with getting good news. but it is the case: this parliament is good news on the occasion of a negotiation completed in record time. the commission is taking the initiative to try to repair one of the biggest problems caused by the covid-19 pandemic: the suspension of schengen and free movement in the european union, thus severely restricting the most cherished fundamental right of european citizenship. take the lead at the end of march. the european parliament votes on its negotiating mandate and the urgency procedure at the end of april. and in record time, at the end of may, we completed a mutually acceptable agreement which allows us today, 8 june, to undertake the final vote and hopefully the final push for entry into force on 1 july, which was what it was all about. a positive message to the european citizens: that this parliament is doing everything it can to relaunch free movement in the schengen area and to do so, in addition, by fulfilling its negotiating mandate, preserving the fundamental rights of europeans, in particular the confidentiality of personal data, complying with the european data protection regulation, and respecting the principle of equality and therefore the prohibition of discrimination, which is also enshrined in the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, in its articles 20 and 21; and to do so with guarantees of legal certainty, therefore, offering clarity and legal certainty where at this moment, or until this moment, there has been obscurity, confusion and legal uncertainty as a result of the accumulation of emergency measures imposed unilaterally by the member states in the severe restriction of fundamental rights, among them the right to free movement and therefore the right to the enjoyment of the schengen area. we have also done so by obtaining in the course of the negotiations a response which the european parliament will hopefully find appropriate, in accordance with the overwhelmingly favourable vote it obtained in the committee responsible, which i have the honour of chairing, on two fundamental issues. the first is to restrict, to save in european law the claimed competence of the member states to impose restrictive measures on free movement despite the regulation and therefore as a legislative act directly binding on the member states, directly applicable from the moment it enters into force and directly enforceable by the citizens before the courts, the principles of proportionality and necessity in any additional measures which the member states may be obliged to impose in order to safeguard public health, which they must announce 48 hours in advance, and which they must also announce to the public, 24 hours before the entry into force of these restrictive measures, in a clear, timely and complete manner what the restriction will consist of, which they must also justify, explain the scope and, above all, set the date of entry into force of this additional measure and, if necessary, its duration, so that the commission, acting in a centralised manner, can inform the european parliament and the other institutions, which are delegated by this regulation, of their right to scrutinise and reserve the right to scrutinise the legal acts provided for in the commission regulation. this regulation finally allows the uniform certification, in accordance with the principles of integrity, authenticity and veracity of data, that the carrier first option has been vaccinated with one of the vaccines recognised by the european medicines agency, in the emergency use list of the world health organisation, second option has obtained a negative test, either pcr or antigen, with sufficient guarantee of reliability in the last few days, in the last 72 hours, or third option has overcome the disease and therefore has an immunological recovery certificate. these three options will therefore provide legal certainty and non-discrimination with a guarantee of protection of fundamental rights. i would like to congratulate the fact that this legislative act has been completed under a european presidency such as the portuguese presidency, which has done its job of trying to bring positions closer together. but now the member states have to do their job. they are the ones who have to ensure in a credible, law-abiding way the technological infrastructure and the ability to provide the certificates as soon as possible to european citizens so that we can restore the free movement of people and the fully functional schengen that we have missed so much during the covid-19 pandemic.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, i must say that it’s a pleasure for me to be here this morning for this debate about the eu digital covid certificate. on 17 march, the commission proposed a european legislative framework covering vaccination, recovery and test certificates. and now, only two and a half months later – a record time – the parliament will be voting on the agreement found with the council. this is the result of a joint commitment by the three institutions to deliver concrete results for eu citizens. with this regulation, every citizen in the european union will have a legal right to receive an eu digital covid certificate whether he or she is vaccinated, tested or recovered. let me use this opportunity to warmly thank the rapporteur, the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe) chair, juan lópez aguilar, and all the shadow rapporteurs, the coordinators of the libe committee, for their efforts and commitment during the successful trilogue negotiations. these were frank and constructive negotiations with a shared objective among all participants. our contacts have been very close throughout the whole process, and i believe that the compromise reflects key points that were essential for the parliament. in particular, the eu digital covid certificate will make it easier for eu citizens to exercise their fundamental right to free movement. the certificate, which will be free of charge, will be issued by all the member states and will have to be accepted across europe. it will contribute to a gradual lifting of restrictions. the eu digital covid certificate will be easily accessible and available to all europeans. moreover, two important principles have been laid down in the text. first, member states will not be able to impose additional travel restrictions on the holders of a eu digital covid certificate, unless such restrictions are necessary and proportionate to safeguard public health. second, where such additional restrictions are imposed on an exceptional basis, for example, because of variants of concern, member states will have to inform the commission and the public in advance. in addition, with the view of reinforcing a coordinated eu approach, the commission has proposed to adapt the council recommendation on the coordination of free movement restrictions in light of this agreement and taking into account these important principles. this will make life easier for citizens. the eu digital covid certificate will help them to move within the eu, be it for work, family or leisure. another important point in our discussion was the affordability of tests. the regulation underlines the importance of universal and affordable tests for all citizens, in particular persons who cross borders on a daily basis. and to support these efforts, the commission has committed to mobilising an additional amount of eur 100 million for tests that qualify for the issuance of eu digital covid test certificate. our discussions have also started an important debate in many member states about the price of tests, and i’m sure that we will see new evolutions in the next weeks about the affordability of the test in the different member states. finally, i would like to make a point on data protection. with its decentralised and offline verification system, the eu digital covid certificate will ensure a very high level of data protection. from the beginning, it was crucial for the commission that the health data of citizens be properly protected. and i believe that the text, further strengthened upon request from the parliament, fully ensures this. ladies and gentlemen, today’s vote is an important milestone. but, for the commission, the work does not end here. together with the member states, we are working day and night to make the eu digital covid certificate a reality on the ground. while the regulation will start to apply from 1 july, we start the roll out already now. the more eu digital covid certificates we can issue in the coming weeks, the more we can avoid problems. we are working hard to ensure a smooth rollout on 1 july. i’m therefore very happy to report that this technical rollout is progressing well. on 1 june, the commission launched the eu gateway, which will host the public keys necessary to verify the certificates, and several member states have already started issuing eu digital covid certificates. more than a million citizens have already received such certificates, and many more will follow in the next weeks and months. looking beyond the eu, the commission is also in contact with third countries to assess the interoperability of their systems with the eu digital covid certificate framework in full compliance with eu data protection rules. in our contacts, we are also promoting the eu certificate as an open and secure international standard. to sum up, with the certificate, citizens will have a practical tool that they can use to travel safely this summer, a tool that they know will be accepted wherever in europe they decide to go. so, i can only encourage you to vote in favour of the agreement found with the council. citizens are counting on us to deliver and with the eu digital covid certificate we show that we are doing so. je voudrais confirmer, monsieur le président, mesdames et messieurs les députés, que, depuis plus de deux mois maintenant, nous avons travaillé sur les trois pistes. d’abord, le certificat. je tiens encore à remercier les négociateurs du parlement pour la manière avec laquelle nous avons pu aboutir, dans le trilogue, à un accord avec le conseil, et je salue le travail de la présidence portugaise dans le cadre de cette négociation. après votre décision, ce certificat sera effectivement en vigueur au 1er juillet prochain. ensuite, dans le même temps, nous avons travaillé sur les outils techniques – comme je l’ai rappelé –, qui sont déjà en train d’être utilisés aujourd’hui par les états membres. la plateforme européenne est d’application depuis le début de ce mois et, tous les jours, des certificats sont déjà émis dans les états membres. et puis nous continuons à travailler avec la présidence portugaise sur la recommandation pour tenter de coordonner au mieux l’ensemble des règles applicables dans l’usage du certificat. le certificat est un outil, in february this year, the european commission adopted a european climate resilience strategy, which rightly notes that climate change has a direct impact on job losses in the fisheries sector. add to this the particularly worrying negative impact that climate change is having on biodiversity and fisheries. i say this as someone who comes from croatia, a maritime country that boasts a centuries-old fishing tradition, and is part of the mediterranean that is particularly vulnerable to climate change today. rising sea temperatures are favouring the emergence of invasive species, and are also having a negative impact on our traditional fisheries by reducing the stock of characteristic species. it directly contributes to the loss of jobs in fisheries and aquaculture. in the adriatic sea 113 invasive species have been recorded, 64 of which have been introduced by human activity. the emergence of 52 species is a consequence of climate change. i would like to mention the emergence of invasive algae of the genus caulerpa whose progressive spread is facilitated by the increase in temperature, which are destroying the biodiversity of the adriatic sea and affecting its economic viability. to protect our fishermen, who are sadly dwindling today, we need to develop a europe that is resilient to climate change. however, the strategy of february lacks concrete proposals on what exactly the european commission plans to do to boost the resilience of fisheries to climate change. in this context, i would like to stress that the european union today has at its disposal an unprecedented regulatory financial instrument that it can use to help the fisheries sector. we must make it clear that this is a sector of strategic importance for the european union, not only in terms of preserving jobs and the economy but also because of the supply of healthy food which is of the utmost importance to us today. we must really help in this regard without delay because the existence of thousands of people depends today on the help of the european union. that is why, here today, on behalf of all the 7,000 croatian fishermen who engage in small-scale coastal fishing, i am asking the european commission to make a concrete commitment to the fight to protect the interests of fishermen and fisheries.
