in writing. first of all, the impact of the covid-19 pandemic on some of the goals of the international conference on population and development on the status of women is evident, due to the disorganization of health care. this will have affected women in africa in particular, as access to neonatal care has become more difficult, even though the continent already has the highest maternal mortality rate in the world. improving the status of women involves addressing certain underlying causes, in particular cultural and religious. former president of niger mahamadou issoufou, for example, pointed out the responsibility of a misinterpretation of islam in early marriage, leading to a real population explosion in the country, which has the highest fertility rate in the world, with almost 7 children per woman in 2016. extended schooling for girls would also reduce their vulnerability to hiv, which is one of the major public health challenges in africa and the world. we must therefore address all these elements without taboos or falsehoods.
in writing. the nairobi summit set out new guidelines to achieve the following concrete goals by 2030: eliminate unmet needs in family planning, end preventable maternal death, end violence and other harmful practices against women and girls. we acknowledge the progress made in specific areas, such as the increased availability of contraceptives or the reduction of maternal and newborn mortality. however, efforts and concrete measures are needed to go further than this. we still have a long way to go. in particular, it is necessary to guarantee: the right to sexual and reproductive health, full access to voluntary termination of pregnancy, freedom of choice for women as regards contraceptive methods, more sexuality education, more information campaigns and awareness-raising campaigns for women on their rights in relation to sexual and reproductive health, continuous training of family planning professionals. and of course, we need to strengthen public health services to ensure that these rights are universal. it is time to ensure that these political commitments are made for all women, girls and young people.
in writing. the international conference on population and development (icpd) in cairo committed to achieving the sustainable development goals by 2030 by ending, inter alia, maternal deaths, violence against women and harmful practices against women and girls. unfortunately, the summit was followed by a resolution containing many controversial elements. it frequently uses the term 'sexual and reproductive health and rights', which is not accepted by international consensus and has no legal definition, and is often interpreted as promoting abortion. it should be noted that there is no international right to abortion. and access to abortion, which under no circumstances can be promoted as a method of family planning, as stated in 8.25 of the icpd, is subject to national law. child marriage, female genital mutilation, limited access to education - the focus should be on combating these harmful practices, not promoting the leftist agenda.
the agenda includes a brief presentation of the report by mr mislav kolakušić on the adequacy, subsidiarity and proportionality of european union legislation - better regulation report for the years 2017, 2018 and 2019 (2020/2262(ini)) (a9-0191/2021).
rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union is a community of free, sovereign and independent states, created by the will of its citizens to work together, on an equal footing, to improve their states and the living standards of their citizens. these facts should never be forgotten. today, we are often witnessing calls for an unfounded increase in the influence and power of the european institutions, especially those not directly elected by the citizens themselves. there are many areas and areas of competence in which the european commission cannot do better and more effectively than the elected representatives of the national states. any unjustified extension of the union's powers, driven by a desire for more power and influence, is detrimental to democracy and, in the long run, to the very survival of the union. the equality of the member states is its paramount value and the very survival of the european union depends on it. without respect for the right of each country to develop and progress within the european union and without respect for our diversity, our common european project is doomed to fail. the report we are putting before you today, members of the european parliament, deals with two key principles of the functioning of the european union. these are the subsidiarity and proportionality principles, by which we have limited and regulated the limits of the european union's competence and defined when and to what extent the union can and should act and when legislation should be left to the member states. the report also contains a very important appeal to all members of parliament, all member states and the citizens of the european union that the union must change and that the power to propose legislation must be given to citizens and their representatives in the european parliament. that is why, with the support of my colleagues, i have included in this report an amendment calling for a revision of the treaty on the functioning of the european union and the introduction of the european parliament's direct right of legislative initiative. the european parliament is the only body of the european union directly elected by its citizens and it represents the authentic will of the citizens of the european union. this report identifies weaknesses in the mechanism for monitoring compliance with the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality, but also offers solutions. better involvement of national parliaments in the legislative process, providing high-quality and understandable explanations of the needs for new legislative initiatives, providing better access to the preparation of legislative texts and, of course, allowing the european parliament to propose laws on its own. respect for the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality, as the foundations of the european union, is a permanent and important obligation of all institutions, because by respecting them, all member states are respected and all citizens of the european union are respected, and they are the founders, the meaning, the purpose of the existence of the european union. the adoption of this report will help to improve the mechanism for monitoring compliance with these principles and to provide citizens with better regulations, which is our fundamental task. thank you to everyone who participated in its creation and i hope we will accept it tomorrow.
dear chairman, dear colleagues, dear mr timmermans, i am so pleased that we are here today to talk about climate and to decide on this important law. because there was a time when we had, under the guise of, more important topics. it's arguably the most important topic today. the environmental question, of course, has become economic but increasingly moral, what kind of world we're going to live in, what kind of condition we're going to leave this planet in for future generations, whether we're thinking short-term or long-term. that's the theme for today. we are the first generation to feel the effects of climate change on our own skin, but we are also the last generation to have a chance to react. to do that, of course, we need quality projects, clear legislation and money. we have all this, we have all this to do and remember: the future is either green or it's not. for that reason, colleagues, i call on you to support this bill and show that we are brave and responsible.

the agenda is as follows: joint debate - recommendation of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs for a second reading on the asylum, migration and integration fund 20212071 (06486/2/2021 c9‐0225/2021 2018/0248 ((cod)) (a9-0224/2021) (rapporteur: tanja fajon) and of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs for a second reading on the integrated border security fund: financial instrument for border security and visa policy 20212027 (06487/2/2021 c9-0226/2021 2018/0249 ((cod)) (a9-0220/2021) (rapporteur: tanja fajon).
the rapporteur. thank you, thank you, chairman, commissioner, and the plenary. today is an important day for europe, for a europe with humane but secure borders and for a europe that is putting solidarity and fair burden-sharing back at the heart of its migration and asylum policies. the new funds will give the union the means to meet ambitious objectives without turning a blind eye to key challenges. we are facing the final step in a long and difficult process of negotiation, of compromise for the common good, not just for europe, but for society as a whole and for european citizens. i would like to begin by thanking my colleagues, the rapporteurs, for their continued support and the negotiators, the european commission and the presidents, germany and portugal, for the constructive approach and the agreement reached. the european union will spend a total of €16 billion over the next seven years on the key areas of border and migration management, almost three times as much as in the previous period. in order to ensure the free movement within the schengen area, we need to manage migration effectively. in order to ensure an area without internal border controls, we must also manage our borders in an efficient and secure manner, but at the same time in a humane manner, with respect for fundamental human rights. the european union will now have the means to do so. there will be no more hiding and pretending. with the new financial instruments, with the new funds, we are also setting much clearer rules for the use of european money for one of the most sensitive, even the most controversial, but at the same time the most important policies in the european union in recent years. we are talking about a policy on migration that has greatly increased mistrust among european governments and mistrust among our citizens. the key is that we have reached a fragile but balanced compromise in the end. after the last migration crisis in 2015, europe has unfortunately still not found effective solutions and a common response on how to move forward. we are still fighting and working for a common migration and asylum policy. although we have already reached a great deal in this very building, the ball is still in the court of the european governments. and the victims of these disagreements within the european union have been innocent people first, many of whom have lost their lives. the member states in the mediterranean are still largely left to their own devices. unfortunately, the schengen area has also become a refugee, and it is not functioning as effectively as it used to. because today many countries still exercise, and have for many years exercised, unjustified, illegal internal border controls. the new financial instruments we are discussing tonight, the new fund, address these very key issues on the basis of solidarity and fair burden-sharing, particularly among the most vulnerable member states, the union will devote more money to resettling and relocating refugees. more money will be allocated to asylum procedures and to the integration of people into society, whereby local communities, which are crucial for successful integration, will be given a greater role through direct funding. on the other hand, the fund will also support the return of people not eligible for international protection in the union to their countries of origin. the second set of measures concerns our borders. the agreement significantly strengthens europe's capacity to manage its borders more effectively and thereby enhances common security. but borders must remain humane, modern, digitised and fluid, as must the future european visa policy. we must therefore also learn from the coronavirus pandemic, we want a europe without borders again. and despite all our efforts, europe's borders, especially its maritime borders, are still very deadly. we cannot allow people to die at the doorstep of europe. we also need to ensure that human rights are respected at all times, including at the border and in border procedures. we must also ensure that human rights are respected at all times at the border and we must not allow illegal and chain-linked returns to become a covert way of managing borders. in doing so, they not only undermine the common migration policy but also allow smugglers to continue to make a fat living at the expense of human tragedies. thanks to the european parliament, to my colleagues who have been with me on this journey, more money will now be allocated to search and rescue operations at sea, but more money will also be allocated to border protection, modernisation and digitalisation. the message of the european parliament to the member states and our citizens tonight is clear. the union is serious, we are serious about the 16 billion euro reasons that european leaders are finally overcoming the blockage of key reforms. there are no excuses. we now have sufficient resources to implement the common policy effectively.
member of the commission. – madam president, this is an achievement, and i warmly thank the rapporteur, ms tanja fajon, and the shadow rapporteurs for their relentless efforts to conclude the negotiations with the council on the asylum, migration and integration fund (amif) and on the instrument for financial support for border management and visa. there are, of course, still a lot of people in crisis, but europe is no longer in a migration crisis. our asylum, migration and integration fund reflects this change. we are moving away from a permanent state of emergency funding to focus on more structural support for migration management, still always putting people first. that doesn’t change. the fund helps member states to help people, mainly by funding internal needs, but also acknowledging needs beyond the eu’s external border, subject to appropriate safeguards, and allowing the new neighbourhood, development, and international cooperation instrument to play its role in migration management. this friday, i will host a high—level forum on resettlement. i want europe to become a global leader on resettlement, helping people in need of international protection to safety. the fund provides the financial firepower to achieve this ambition, creating incentives for member states to effectively resettle people and allowing member states to organise projects to support refugees. the fund supports legal pathways to europe, helping talent to thrive and our businesses to prosper. the fund supports integration and inclusion by helping people to build new homes and new lives in europe. we will work closely with other eu funds, such as the european social fund plus (esf+), to maximise synergies and make a real difference for people on the ground. the fund also supports dignified returns of those not eligible for asylum that have to return to their country of origin. in short, this agreement on the amif is another important step towards a more european way of managing migration. as the examples i just mentioned show, it will play a strategic role in the implementation of the new pact on migration and asylum. the effective protection of the eu’s external border is crucial if we are to manage the challenges ahead. the instrument for financial support for border management and visa policy will support strong and smart borders. it will facilitate legitimate border crossings, while preventing irregular migration and cross-border crime. it will support the good functioning of the schengen area without internal border checks, a topic we will discuss more in the next debate, and support a higher internal security level. over the past few years, a number of measures have been introduced to address the immediate challenges and to put in place the important building blocks needed to ensure strong and smart borders. the funds will support the continuation of this work by ensuring that large—scale it systems used for border management are robust and can function seamlessly with one another and with national systems, and by ensuring that the eu’s common visa policy adapts to evolving security concerns, challenges linked to migration and new opportunities offered by technological developments. the fund will contribute to enhance the member states’ capacities in these areas, reinforce cooperation and harmonisation between member states, and create synergies between national authorities and frontex. the fund will be providing for a minimum level of expenditure of 10% in the common visa policy to ensure a harmonised approach with regard to the issuance of visas. this will facilitate travel. it will also build trust by helping to ensure a similar level of protection of the borders. it will address the current differences between the member states due to geographical location and differences in capacities and resources available. the increased budget, amounting to eur 6.3 billion, will allow us to meet unforeseen needs and new priorities in a better way. our eu funds will help us to put our policies into practice, help us to manage migration and restore schengen to free movement. we now have seven years ahead of us to translate into actions the opportunities and support captured in these two funds. the commission will update parliament and the council on the developments, and counts on your continued support throughout the implementation period.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, dear commissioner, year after year, survey after survey, we know that migration is one of the topics that is most important to our citizens, a topic that europeans expect the european union to take seriously and to address at european level. this new fund for asylum, migration and integration is a very important first step to show that we have received that message. we tripled the size of the budget to almost 10 billion to also really reflect the magnitude of the challenges that we are facing. these challenges are diverse. member states need our assistance to fight illegal migration, to make solidarity a reality, to ensure good reception conditions, to effectively integrate refugees and to make sure that those who cannot stay are also returned to their country of origin. and yes, this is in essence an internal eu fund, but we must not make the mistake of ignoring the external factors of migration. we need to work with countries of origin and transit to fight smuggling networks together, to support reintegration projects and to offer perspectives in the region. i am happy that the majority of this parliament ultimately agreed with the epp to allow this fund to have the flexibility to do so, because if there’s anything we’ve learned in the past seven years, it is that the future is not always predictable, so we need this flexibility also to be able to respond swiftly to the challenges of the future. and all we need is political willingness in the member states not only to spend the money under this fund, which is fairly easy to do, but to finally take political responsibility to break the deadlock in the council and to agree on a common european asylum system.
madam president, let me be clear from the outset. the immigration package without solidarity between member states is not acceptable. we cannot allow a small number of countries to fall back on european values and principles of solidarity, keeping us all hostage and preventing us from moving forward, with the consequence that peripheral countries, like malta, are left to fend for themselves on immigration. this is unacceptable! one of the four pillars of the asylum, immigration and integration fund is solidarity itself and the fund ensures that member states most affected by immigration find the rest of the european union with them, in solidarity and with common policies and systems to face the challenges together. clearly, this is not done - the only thing done is to solve the problem with money, but this does not solve anything. while the intention of financial aid is very good, the solution is not more money, but solidarity in practice.
on behalf of the left group. madam president, this fund should be a key structural tool to support asylum and integration. it should allow funds to be received and managed by town halls and municipalities without any obstacles. it should be a fund that encourages policies of regularization. it should be a lot more than it is. and although it includes positive issues such as support for real solidarity between member states, humanitarian resettlement and the explicit mention of ngos in the implementation of funds, we cannot support with our vote a fund which calls itself asylum and integration, but which includes funding for the outsourcing of borders to third countries and returns. because integration and deportation are contradictory elements and because a policy focused on the externalization of borders and deportation we already know very well where it ends: in the systematic violation of human rights. and they don't have our vote for that.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, fellow citizens of the european union, europe is being invaded by several million illegal immigrants. this is the result of the profit-making ambitions of the big industrialists of germany, italy and france, who, driven by their desire for enormously cheap labor, have brought europe to the brink of conflict. millions of illegal immigrants are entering europe. all the countries of the european union are breaking their own rules. illegal border crossings are not punished. all citizens and all countries of europe have strict laws about it. there are prison sentences, large fines. we follow our own rules and we don't have illegal immigration. you who want cheap labor from the middle east and africa, introduce charters. and don't bully countries that don't have enough jobs for their own citizens.
the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european union's external borders is based on the principle of subsidiarity. european funds strengthen and direct our common fight against illegal migration, but also against cross-border crime and terrorism. countries on the external border, such as croatia, are thus equipping their police with the most modern technical equipment, increasing security while fully respecting national and european regulations. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's policy on asylum and return, in particular by ensuring that the union's asylum system is as transparent as possible, and that the union's asylum system is as transparent as possible. i congratulate the rapporteur on this success but the next step must be a comprehensive agreement on a european policy on migration and asylum.
the migration crisis and then the pandemic have shown the vulnerability of schengen. freedom of movement without internal borders is a symbol of european integration that all european citizens should enjoy. with a modernised police force, croatia is now fulfilling its duty to all european citizens by protecting the longest external land border, responsibly and lawfully and, more recently, with independent border control supervision, the single precedent mechanism provided for in the immigration act. the commission therefore unequivocally confirms that croatia is ready for schengen and the parliament sends a strong message that all europeans should enjoy equal freedom of movement. accession to schengen will bring croatian citizens the equality they deserve, facilitate economic and tourism development and ensure greater security, and this is in the interest not only of croatia but of the entire european union.
dear chair, ladies and gentlemen, although the pandemic is not yet over, europe is slowly opening up and entering a new period, entering a new era. vaccination is progressing, though not always at the rate we would like. the economy is opening up and after months of closure, thanks to covid digital certificates, europe's citizens are once again traveling more easily. this is also a prerequisite for a successful tourist season on which more than 23 million europeans depend for their livelihoods, a sector that accounts for over a tenth of the total european economy and suffered a decline of over 70% during the pandemic. at the same time, europe has a responsibility towards less developed countries. vaccine donation and solidarity towards the countries of the western balkans, our neighbours and other regions, is unparalleled globally but can still be stronger because we will only get out of this crisis together. the commission should also be able to provide the necessary information to the member states to enable them to take appropriate measures to ensure the effective implementation of the national recovery plans. we need to focus on the future, on new digital technologies, but also on our core values: freedom and the dignity of every human person. we have a shared responsibility to prepare europe for a new age and to prepare for a post-pandemic world.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, i would like to thank you for your speeches to remind us how we were able to deploy the digital covid certificate and, in a way, to celebrate this deployment. but as you have said, it is clear that work to ensure the proper functioning of the system will continue. and i want to say some words on different questions raised during the debate. first of all, about the phasing-in to implement a certificate. all the member states are connected to the gateway. so that's the first element, a positive one. the commission has received a couple of notifications from member states, which mostly concern small delays, or delays concerning specific types of certificate. seven member states have informed us that the issuing of their certificate is not yet fully ready. slovakia will start issuing test certificates based on rapid antigen tests as of 9 july. finland will start issuing test and recovery certificates as of 14 july. malta will start issuing test and recovery certificates as of also 14 july like in finland. denmark will start issuing eu digital covid certificates to persons without a danish registration number and in a non-digital format from 12 august at the latest. spain will make use of the phasing-in period until eu certificates can be issued throughout its territory. sweden is not issuing test and recovery certificates as of 1 july and will inform us when it will be ready. ireland is not issuing an eu certificate as of 1 july, they will also inform us, but the authorities are working with 19 july as the date where the issuance of the certificate should start. about the possible frauds: we are aware about reports of fraud. we take this issue very seriously. you know that europol has been following the issue of pandemic-related fraud since the beginning and is monitoring the developments closely. it is important to distinguish between the security of the eu certificate itself and the forgery of vaccination certificates that are used to generate secure eu digital certificates. the eu certificate is secure. it contains a qr code with security features that cannot be falsified and pass a verification check. this is why the commission has set up a system that allows the verification of the authenticity of qr codes across the union. it is very important that member states take appropriate measures to ensure that points where certificates are generated are well protected. it is also important that vaccination proof is properly checked before a secure eu digital covid certificate is generated. the falsification of documents is illegal and punishable under our national laws. i will continue to work on it in close collaboration with the member states. as regards the requests concerning families, i would nevertheless like to point out that we have, in the revised recommendation adopted by the council, provided for the exemption of children under 12 from testing, which makes it much easier for families to travel when the parents are vaccinated. testing is only required after the age of 12. of course, we will continue to look with the member states at how to make testing more and more affordable. i have said that the 100 million we have already raised are being distributed to the 24 member states that have applied for the scheme. there are also steps, of course, being taken in the member states to facilitate access to testing. i would like to stress that we will continue to promote vaccination campaigns. several of you mentioned that there were still delays in some vaccination campaigns. there is a major effort to be made in this area. we will of course continue to coordinate with the member states. it is quite logical that there are concerns about the development of certain variants. what we want to do is to try to coordinate the approach also with regard to these variants. the regulation therefore provides that member states wishing to take new restrictive measures must inform the commission and the other member states. and we obviously want to avoid any disproportionate or discriminatory measures and in particular, as i said, we are trying to avoid travel bans, as we had to try to avoid them earlier this year by intervening with a number of member states. i would also like to draw the attention of your parliament, madam president, to the fact that coordination applies to the member states, but also to all the institutions. you know - well, some of you know, especially at libe - we have an important trilogue on friday. i was surprised to receive a request to take a rapid antigen test before joining the european parliament in brussels, even though i have a full vaccination certificate. i think coordination should take place on all floors. i thought that the certificate could be used everywhere, as you requested, including perhaps the premises of the european parliament. so there is still work for everyone, i think, in this area. i also want to say a few words about the third countries, and of course we are open to working with our partners around the world. we have received a number of requests from different countries. this includes, for example, albania, bosnia and herzegovina, israel, japan, south korea, kosovo, montenegro, north macedonia, serbia, taiwan, turkey, jordan, the united arab emirates, mongolia, thailand and kazakhstan. we have more concrete discussions already with andorra, australia, canada, malaysia, moldova, monaco, morocco, new zealand, san marino, singapore, ukraine, the uk, the us and the vatican. of course, about the us, we are continuing our exchange with the us administration. it was possible to discuss already during the visit of president biden. we have continued bilateral contacts with the secretary for homeland security and we will continue to see if it's possible to have a real positive reaction. as the council agreed in its latest revision of the council recommendation on travel to the european union, reciprocity should be taken into account on a case-by-case basis, where member states decide to lift restrictions for travellers in possession of valid proof of a covid-19 certificate. but also we try to see if it's possible to have a positive evolution in a very short bit of time with different partners everywhere again in the world. i would simply like to confirm, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, that the certificate is to help us to facilitate in a concrete way the free movement of persons during the pandemic. but i would also like to remind you, as you all know, that we are of course looking forward to the day when we can once again move freely without restrictions. this will be done as soon as the health situation allows. and on a more personal note, i hope that in the summer that is about to begin, the certificate will allow you to travel to europe and, given the work done in recent months, to enjoy a well-deserved holiday.
the debate is closed. written statements (rule 171)
in writing. on 1 july, the european union digital covid certificate regulation came into force with the aim of helping to restore freedom of movement within the eu, a right severely restricted after the declaration of the pandemic. from that date, member states should be prepared to issue and verify the physical or electronic document certifying that persons have been vaccinated, tested or have recovered from the disease. the regulation provides, however, for a period of six weeks in which member states may prepare to issue the certificates referred to in the regulation. twenty-one of them, as well as norway, iceland and liechtenstein, had already started issuing certificates before 1 july, and five more started doing so as from that date. however, according to the european commission's data of 1 july, six member states are still not prepared for its implementation (hungary, ireland, malta, the netherlands, romania and sweden), which will require redoubling efforts in this respect.
in writing. on 1 july, the european union digital covid certificate regulation came into force with the aim of helping to restore freedom of movement within the eu, a right severely restricted after the declaration of the pandemic. from that date, member states should be prepared to issue and verify the physical or electronic document certifying that persons have been vaccinated, tested or have recovered from the disease. the regulation provides, however, for a period of six weeks in which member states may prepare to issue the certificates referred to in the regulation. twenty-one of them, as well as norway, iceland and liechtenstein, had already started issuing certificates before 1 july, and five more started doing so as from that date. however, according to the european commission's data of 1 july, six member states are still not prepared for its implementation (hungary, ireland, malta, the netherlands, romania and sweden), which will require redoubling efforts in this respect.
in writing. the european union has approached the challenges posed by covid-19 on the basis of solidarity and community coordination. we have created and adopted the eu funds for economic recovery with unprecedented speed, and now, following the acceleration of vaccination campaigns, we need to make the free movement of citizens accessible again. the eu digital covid certificate was created for this purpose and came into force on 1 july, a week after the extraordinary speed of the emergency procedure. under the regulation, the certificate is valid in all eu member states as well as in norway, liechtenstein and iceland and certifies, among other things, the vaccination, the occurrence of the disease or the negative test result of the individual. the certificate should be made available free of charge to all eu citizens. as a hungarian citizen, i have unfortunately found that the digital security certificate issued by the orbán government is not suitable for ensuring free travel, as in many cases an error occurs when reading it. without a properly functioning eu digital covid certificate, it is difficult to enter other european countries and the required testing in many cases imposes extra costs and burdens on hungarian citizens. it is unacceptable that the orbán government is forcing hungarian citizens into a disadvantaged position with its special-way policy again! as european hungarians, we demand the speedy resolution of the technological problems so that all european citizens can regain their right to free movement!

in writing. the digital covid certificate is a major european achievement that is now producing results. in a short time we have managed to be consistent with the demands of european citizens and to offer them a pan-european system to facilitate free movement within the union. greece is one of the first countries to successfully implement the certificate since may and now the whole of europe is benefiting from the implementation of this digital tool, giving priority to the safety of cross-border movements. i would like to emphasise once again that the european covid certificate, launched on the initiative of the greek prime minister kyriakos mitsotakis, was the most appropriate solution for a safe exit from the pandemic ahead of the summer season and is now available free of charge in all eu languages. today we are doubly proud as greeks and as europeans, because we have proved that we can, in difficult circumstances, have the answers our citizens are asking for and apply them for the common good. as vaccinations progress across the eu, we can feel safe and free to organise our summer holidays without restrictions with the new european covid certificate.
the next item is the debate on the report by nicolás gonzález casares, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council on a reinforced role for the european medicines agency in crisis preparedness and management for medicinal products and medical devices (com(2020)0725 – c9-0365/2020 – 2020/0321(cod)) (a9-0216/2021).
member of the commission. – madam president, we are into the 19th month of the covid-19 pandemic. even when looking at this number, it makes me realise how much has happened in the last 19 months that has changed all our lives. the european medicines agency has been absolutely central to our collective effort to tackle it. to us, from the very early days of this pandemic, it was clear that we needed to look and to see how we could do things differently. the necessity of a stronger mandate for the european medicines agency became apparent very quickly. we needed to permanently monitor the shortages of critical medicines, we need to report on the risk of shortages of medical devices during crisis and to catalyse the development and the faster approval of medicines with fast-track scientific advice and rolling reviews. we need to act rapidly and we need to act together as a union. i want to thank parliament and all your efforts to reach an agreement on the european medicines agency proposal. this is a proposal which is part of a package of three initiatives that aim to build a strong european health union, and together with the proposal to strengthen the european centre for disease prevention and control (ecdc) and our cross-border health threats framework, this package is our collective response to learn from the current pandemic and be better prepared for future crises. and as the honourable member just said, we need to be proactive, not only reactive, to public health crises. honourable members, it’s our responsibility that people have access to critical medicines and medical devices during current and future crises. throughout this pandemic, i want to show you and share with you that the european medicines agency really stepped up its work considerably, but based on temporary and on exceptional measures so that we could have the type of commitment and we could meet the many challenges we were faced with. and they have done a wonderful, wonderful job. but we now need to put this arrangement on a permanent footing. we need to stabilise these ad hoc structures we created that will catalyse the development and the faster approval of medicines to treat or prevent disease causing a public health crisis. it’s for these reasons that these negotiations need to move forward and quickly to a conclusion. the progress made so far by parliament and council on the european medicines agency proposal is a welcome step in the right direction and i want to thank you for your engagement and for your very active contribution. i welcome the fact that the amendments tabled by parliament retain the level of ambition of the original proposal and do not mark a significant departure from its policy objective, and i share that level of ambition with you. but there are just two points i want to raise. some of the amendments put forward by parliament should be looked at again. this applies particularly to the delay of the date of application for medical devices by one year and the creation of a european medicine supply database. if we defer the rules and the application of the rules for medical devices, then we are also at the risk of deferring the enhanced level of protection for citizens. and the medicines supply database goes far beyond the scope of the proposal and would result in duplication and substantial additional costs. so instead, we need to maximise synergies with existing national systems so that our efforts are as efficient and effective as possible. honourable members, a stronger european medicines agency is a centrepiece of a strong european health union. we are building its mandate and it must be strengthened as we look at the pandemic as a stronger, wiser, more resilient and better prepared union. we are called to demonstrate that we can move quickly and decisively to tackle the challenges facing us and to strengthen the structures and systems that protect our health. we have already done it together on eu4health and on the digital covid-19 certificates and the council has already made considerable progress and adopted its general approach on 15 june. now, as we start the interinstitutional negotiations under the slovenian presidency, let me assure you that the commission will play its part in reaching a quick and effective agreement, and you can count on my support. together, we can translate these proposals into concrete and long-lasting protection for citizens in times of crisis. we need a strong european health union, a health union which will deliver nothing more and nothing less than what citizens deserve and what citizens expect from us. and this is not about taking away competences. it’s about being better prepared, and it’s about standing up to what we now need to do. and you can count on me being there with the level of ambition that you are pushing forward with as well.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, i would like to start by thanking mr gonzález casares, the rapporteur, for the good cooperation, and commissioner kyriakides for bringing forward this parliament and for the epp, my political group. indeed, the proposal of an eu medicines supply database would be a very useful additional measure to help address eu shortages. but the proposal in this on behalf of the renew group. madam president, madam commissioner, it has been more than a year now since the european medicines agency has been operating on the edge of the treaty in responding to the covid-19 pandemic. a year of ad hoc solutions, and for one clear reason: to save lives. because, of course, all that matters is saving lives. this also explains why we, the negotiators from the political groups, wanted to get this legislative proposal on the ground as quickly as possible. i would like to thank our rapporteur, mr nicolás gonzález casares, for this excellent cooperation between us. the agency will now provide scientific advice at the earliest stages of drug development, to work with manufacturers and thus accelerate the availability of treatments and vaccines. it will coordinate clinical trials across the union to build on the data available. it will continuously monitor the state of stocks of medicines and will therefore be able to identify those shortages much more quickly, which we are in fact defining with recommendations to remedy. the working method is also important - transparency, again. partnering doctors, patients, manufacturers: here again, it is the sacred union that must prevail to accelerate our response to health crises, including, of course, with hera, our future health emergency preparedness and response authority. this strengthened medicines agency will be one of the pillars of the global pandemic response. with this clear hope that the world will be ready in less than 100 days for the next crisis. this is the commitment of the last g7 meeting in carbis bay.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i am very glad about the work that we have done and i would like to congratulate the rapporteur and all my colleagues that worked to reinforce the role of the european medicines agency (ema). many citizens probably did not even know about the existence of the ema before the outbreak of the pandemic, but i think that today many of us have realised that its role is crucial to make sure that the treatments we take, such as medicines and vaccines, work well and are safe. as of today, the ema will be stronger and will be able to help countries in a more efficient and faster way. first of all, it will put a much stronger focus on prevention and on the monitoring of shortages. the pandemic showed us indeed the importance of securing the supply of medicines and medical devices, because their shortages can have a dramatic impact on the treatments and therefore also on patients’ health. companies will now have to submit plans where they explain how they would react in case they face problems during the supply chain and they will get penalties in case they omit information that can be useful to the authorities. this will increase transparency in the process and will allow timely interventions to mitigate possible shortages. we also worked to create a webpage that will be publically available, so that patients, healthcare professionals, but also every citizen, will be able to get information about the shortages in real time. another new element will be the creation of a list of essential products that authorities will consider as critical during a health crisis: this will be important to monitor their demand and the supply and take actions to ensure their availability. however, i do not think it is a good idea that companies have their say on which products should be considered as essential as it is clear that as manufacturers they would have an interest in promoting a product over another one. so i would invite my colleagues to think a little bit more about this and exclude companies from this process. apart from this, i think overall we did a very good job because we are reinforcing an important agency that in future will help us to act immediately and act together to fight viruses and diseases that, as we saw, will not stop at the borders.

madam president, i would like to thank you. the pandemic is far from over. we have a lot of work to do to vaccinate the world, but we also have to start our work on pandemics, on future health risks. neither the eu nor the member states were prepared for the crisis we are now experiencing. it is certainly no understatement to say so. today we are taking an important step towards a health union, towards stronger emergency preparedness, and it is really about time. the creation of the european medicines agency is an important part of that work. we have a very strong agreement on the table. it includes the fact that we are strengthening the prevention work, so that we can be one step ahead when the next pandemic comes. we're increasing transparency so we can better monitor the fda's actions. we are also opening up the possibility of including veterinary medicinal products in the future, because of course sick animals should also be able to receive the right medicines. we're also tackling what was one of the biggest problems at the beginning of the pandemic, which was the shortage of medicines and medical products like protective equipment. it was heartbreaking when they were stopped on the way to their important use. for our value chains to be as interconnected as they are today, we need to ensure that a shortage in one place does not cost a life in another. with a common drug database, we can ensure that we never again end up where we were last spring, where people died because we didn't have the right medicines or the right equipment in place. avoiding empty pharmaceutical stocks will be another key task of the newly re-equipped agency. i would like to conclude by thanking our s&d rapporteur, mr nicolás gonzález casares, for a very good agreement and wish you good luck in the negotiations with the member states.

on behalf of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the two texts presented today are part of the larger health package that the covid crisis has accelerated and, above all, pushed to extremes. because, in order to find solutions to an unprecedented crisis that no one in europe saw coming and, above all, whose management was chaotic, the european union is proposing regulations with potentially worrying effects. in addition to strengthening the role of the ema in managing drug shortages, on which member states have great difficulty in agreeing, we are proposed to strengthen the infectious diseases surveillance agency. however, it was the same one that delayed 22 days before speaking out about the new pandemic, denying its risks until mid-march 2020. a copy-and-paste model of the who, which has failed and whose dire consequences are well known. and now it is being asked to coordinate the response systems of each member state without increasing either the human or the financial resources. similarly, we are being offered a new regulation on serious crossborder threats to health, which is nothing more or less than the dogmatic repetition of elements already dealt with in other texts on stock management, computer databases, trade transparency and, above all, the confirmation of the denial of the protective role of borders. the latter text is, in our opinion, particularly dangerous in that it lays the foundations for the future era agency, modelled on the american barda, to combat the risks of bacteriological warfare. but in the end, it is the weakening of article 168 of the feu treaty, on public health, that is likely to occur, thus seizing all national prerogatives in health matters and without, on the contrary, bringing any added value. this is yet another exploitation of the crisis for the benefit of the europeans. that is why we find these two texts unacceptable.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, the covid—19 pandemic has unveiled new and unexpected threats to public health, but also highlighted and exacerbated existing weaknesses in the healthcare system of many member states as well as beyond eu borders. it has demonstrated how a public health problem can affect the proper functioning of all european sectors and has shown that efforts to combat health emergencies require better coordination at eu level. looking at this from an internal market perspective, the regulation on serious cross—border threats to health issues raises three important issues: supply chains, procurement and data sharing. mainly, the pandemic has exposed the limited diversity of suppliers and over—reliance on a few particular supply chains. such vulnerabilities need to be addressed by encouraging broader participation of small and medium—sized enterprises in joint procurement procedures. the eu should pay particular attention to providing technical assistance and reducing administrative burdens, to boost smes’ engagements in the process. last but not least, the new regulation should ensure that data sets are equally accessible and usable across the eu for better coordination. i do hope that the proposed solution will contribute to the improvement of operational coordination in the eu. in this regard, i support the creation of the health emergency response and preparedness authority.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, the absence of references to public health services in these reports is striking. public services that were key in the covid-19 response are weighed down by the systemic public disinvestment policies imposed by neoliberal policies and economic and political constraints of the european union on member states. it is not surprising, given the objectives surrounding the so-called european health union, that the european health market does not aim at the capacity building and the necessary strengthening of public health services, but rather seeks to open up the field to projects of liberalization, privatization and concentration of this sector. at the same time, it insists on a public procurement model that seeks to condition the ability of states to diversify the procurement of medicines, the option that blocked the faster progress of vaccination and that tied states to the interests of multinational pharmaceutical companies, preventing the acquisition of vaccines recognized by the world health organization. the response to emerging health situations cannot be a pretext for the transfer of competences from states to the supranational sphere. what is needed is the strengthening of public health services and their universal and free character, and the empowerment of states in public research, in the production of medicines and other medical products, eliminating dependencies on the outside world. the strengthening of cooperation in the field of health must be promoted on the basis of respect for the sovereignty of states and their autonomy, by valuing their national authorities and taking as a reference the public interests and that of the populations.
mr president, the pandemic has undoubtedly been a huge human tragedy, with social and economic consequences that we all know. however, the union has learned valuable lessons for the future. among them, the need to make progress in building a common european health policy, in the specific areas where this greater integration will enable us to do more and better. this regulation on crossborder threats is of course one of the pillars of this new european health union and we are pleased with the quantity and quality of the contributions we have been able to make. these include the definition of real health needs in all policies, the extension of epidemiological surveillance of communicable diseases to their consequences on noncommunicable diseases, in particular mental health, the inclusion of regions, especially cross-border regions, in prevention and transparency plans, and the publication of the list of members of the eu health security committee to avoid conflicts of interest with the pharmaceutical industry. in conclusion, more europe in the field of health so that the member states and regions of the union can guarantee more and better the right to health of their citizens.
mr president, i would like to thank you. hopefully, we are now moving toward brighter times with less intense intensive care and relaxed restrictions. however, it is now that we must learn the lessons of the pandemic. because around the corner, there's not only a risk of a fourth wave or new mutations of the virus, but it's also about our way of life, in imbalance with nature, which increases the risk of future pandemics. international cooperation, including within the eu, is now required if we are to avoid ever having to relive what we have been through in the last 18 months. at the beginning of the summer, we voted in the european parliament for a strengthened pharmaceutical authority. this will help avoid the shortage of protective equipment and medicines that existed at the beginning of the pandemic. today, however, we are taking the next step towards a european health union. in particular, we are strengthening preventive work with an eu-wide early warning system, regardless of where the next contagious disease may occur. the cover-up of the pandemic that prevailed in the beginning, by china, and which slowed down important measures at an early stage, must never happen here in the eu. in addition to prevention, we now have new tools to monitor and predict the evolution of future viruses in real time. we also support a new global framework for cooperation against pandemics, within the framework of the world health organisation. whether antibiotic resistance, vaccine resistance or unhealthy animal husbandry is behind the next pandemic, the eu must always be armed and ready. this will now become more possible as we take a stand for cooperation on crossborder health threats and a well-equipped european disease control authority. we have enough crises to deal with. let pandemics be a thing of the past.

mr president, europe is more divided today than it has been in the last 30 years. today, europeans are divided into those who trust politicians in power and who have ingested pharmaceutical products against covid-19, believing that they are less of a danger to them than the disease itself, even though it was almost impossible to find a person under 70 who died from covid-19. on the other hand, there are citizens who rightly believe that vaccines are incomparably more dangerous than covid-19 itself, especially those under the age of 50. one million and two hundred thousand side effects, fourteen thousand deaths were reported in the european union, the united kingdom and the united states alone.
the commission has also received a number of requests for information from the member states. in such circumstances, as many as 66% of our citizens think that the european union should have more powers in the field of health. yet, even within its existing powers, the european union can and must do more to tackle health threats that transcend national borders. it is with the regulation on serious cross-border threats to health that we are creating a european health union and protecting our citizens from current and future health threats. it is now clear that the world health organization was late in declaring a pandemic. the adoption of this regulation gives us the instrument to declare a public health emergency independently of the world health organisation. as rapporteur for the european people's party in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, i proposed a mechanism for prior authorisation by the european commission to introduce export restrictions on medical countermeasures. i am proud that this proposal was adopted as part of this report. the commission should be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 21 to supplement this regulation by laying down detailed rules for the application of the measures referred to in article 4 of regulation (eu) no 514/2014. the introduction of this mechanism makes it impossible for the functioning of our health systems to be compromised at the critical moment of a health emergency. the pandemic has shown us that health threats like infectious diseases know no borders. building on the lessons learned, we are now building a safe and healthy resilient european union for the benefit of all its citizens.
rapporteur. – mr president, we are entering the peak of the budgetary procedure for the next annual budget of the eu. there is still a lot of uncertainties in front of us, so we will have to remain presidency. it is especially crucial to use additional appropriations in certain key areas: support the driving force of the european recovery and economy, small and medium enterprises. secondly, digital, green and secure europe. with these aims, we propose to fully reuse the decommitments in the field of research as there is an unexpected amount of decommitments in 2020, which was not part of mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the european union is the world's largest importer and exporter of food. yet, it is frightening that as many as 33 million people in the european union cannot afford a quality meal every other day. this is particularly worrying when we know that as much as 20% of the food produced is wasted. on the other hand, there is the growing problem of obesity. more than half of the adult population is overweight, and in this context it is estimated that in 2017, more than 950 000 people died in the eu as a result of an unhealthy diet, mainly due to cardiovascular disease and cancer. in addition, the covid-19 pandemic has demonstrated the fragility of the supply chain in the face of health threats. the key is therefore to ensure a transition to a sustainable food system, and this will only be possible with a fair implementation of the farm-to-table strategy. as rapporteur for the european people's party's farm-to-table strategy in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, i want this strategy to ensure that citizens have access to healthy, varied and affordable food. it must not, however, lead to a reduction in production and food shortages, nor should the cost of its implementation be borne by consumers, which unfortunately is a real danger today. it also means protecting our farmers from unfair competition from third countries with products of inferior quality, and seeing farmers as partners to be helped, not as enemies, as some political groups in this parliament unfortunately do. finally, only a fully informed consumer can make the right decision when buying food products. so i was very much in favour of putting the nutrition labels on the front of the pack, rather than the small print that was often used on the back, which unfortunately, at the time, consumers very often could not read. finally, i am pleased that we have the opportunity today to speak about the european union's contribution to the transformation of global food systems. however, our contribution to this transformation begins with the european union itself moving towards a sustainable food system.

in writing. – on 23 september 2021, un secretary—general antónio guterres will convene a food systems summit as part of the decade of action to achieve the sustainable development goals (sdgs) by 2030 . the summit will awaken the world to the fact that we must all work together to transform the way the world produces, consumes and thinks about food, interconnecting all 17 sdgs, focusing on those relating to eradicating poverty and hungry, mainstreaming the gender dimension and innovation, in a climate—neutral world . the covid—19 pandemic is a wake—up call for change . the crisis has underlined the fact that getting food ‘from farm to fork’, as the eu has launched the strategy, cannot be taken for granted and has demonstrated the interconnectedness of actors and activities in eco—agriculture and throughout the sustainable food system . it has also made us acutely aware of the vulnerabilities of certain groups and sectors and of the inequalities affecting the food supply chain . the post—covid recovery represents an opportunity to ‘build back better’ . more sustainable, fairer and inclusive food systems should contribute to a well—being economy that works for people and the planet, leaving no one behind .
the next item is the debate on the request for an oral answer to the commission on the reversal of the negative social consequences of the covid-19 pandemic by lucia ďuriš nicholsonová, dennis radtke, manuel pizarro, atidzhe alieva-veli, elena lizzi, katrin langensiepen, elżbieta rafalska and leila chaibi, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs [2021/2734 ((rsp)) ] (o-000044/2021 - b9-0032/21).
member of the commission. – mr president, indeed, the pandemic has had a dramatic impact on our economies and societies. it has exposed and deepened existing inequalities, some of them inherited from the previous financial crisis. but this time, faced with this unprecedented crisis, the eu has reacted swiftly and boldly. swift and coordinated action by the eu and the member states helped protect lives and incomes. and this time – and this makes the real difference – europe has shown solidarity. while the eu and national action has significantly cushioned the social impact of the pandemic, vulnerable groups have indeed been hit disproportionately, highlighting the need to have adequate and well-performing social protection systems. as we want to rebuild in a more sustainable and resilient way we need to ensure that the recovery is fair and inclusive. the european pillar of social rights is our guiding compass in this endeavour. i remember the new headline targets for 2030 on employment, adult learning and poverty reduction, and i try to mention a certain number of measures which show that we had in mind precisely more vulnerable groups, and among the vulnerable groups are women, because the majority of the frontline workers were women. in spite of that, and this has been recalled this morning by the president, they are underpaid, badly paid, their work is not recognised as it should be, and therefore we have made a proposal on pay transparency, because applauding the frontline workers, applauding the women who every day try to secure our health system, is not enough. the second one is the young – especially the young – and this was also mentioned this morning by the president, with a new initiative especially addressing the difficult situation of the less-favoured young – those who might not be at university, not in apprenticeships, who might be unemployed, very often the so-called neets – to give them new opportunities, and therefore we have launched a strengthened, a better youth guarantee, which addresses particularly their problems and helps to bring them back into the labour market and bring them back also for being skilled. a european child guarantee. this now has to be implemented. this is crucial because we know that children have suffered a lot, especially disadvantaged or children from disadvantaged backgrounds, from this pandemic, and therefore it’s very important now to implement the child guarantee swiftly also with the european funds which have been included in the esf+. we have proposed a dynamic active labour market policy, which we call ease, effective active support to employment, precisely to bring people back into a job in a job market, a labour market, which is changing, and now we discover there is unemployment, on the one hand, and there are a lot of shortages on the labour market in some countries, so we have really to help people and especially also young people, vulnerable workers, to find a job, and here active labour market policies through reskilling, job-to-job transition and hiring incentives are extremely important. next. occupational safety and health strategy to protect workers because we all know that one of the places with the highest risk is the working place, and the directive on adequate minimum wages is also a way to value work and help those who cannot make a decent living out of their work and their wage. we will continue on that. we will work on the minimum income recommendation. we will also try to have an initiative on distributional impact assessment of the rrf, but also in the context of the fit for 55. question number 2, the data. yes, i agree, we need the right data, we need better data, we need more precise data, to have an evidence-based policy, and this means that we have to work on and improve also the collection of data with eurostat and the member states, and the regional dimension in that context is also very important. on the rrf. i think this is a major achievement and this has been very much underlined this morning by the president, and here also it is clear that member states have understood that the rrf is an extremely important tool to improve social conditions because the consequence of the pandemic has been economic, it has been financial, but it has also been mainly social, and therefore a lot of funding, about 30% of the funds of the rrf, are allocated to social objectives, these being education, health, bringing people back into jobs. all this is extremely important. so i think we will monitor this, obviously, we will see what will be the impact of using the rrf money. the money is certainly in the esf+, precisely focused also on vulnerable groups, but also in investeu. we have eur 2.8 billion for investeu. we have to promote social investment. this goes along with reforms, this goes along with structural reforms, and therefore we need better instruments precisely to foster and to promote this social investment. sure has been mentioned. yes, sure is a big success. we have helped a lot of people to keep their jobs. we talk about 30 million. but what for me is very important is that through the sure instrument we did not just innovate – because it’s the first time social bonds of some kind were created – but we helped all the countries, all the member states, to introduce a short-time work scheme, which they did. every member state introduced a short-time work scheme, so there was not this kind of inequality – some doing it, some not being able for financial reasons to do it – and this kept europe together and improved the situation of our labour market europe-wide. certainly, we will continue evaluating sure, 94% of the money has been allocated. now we look at how it has been used and certainly in september we will come back with a new report on the impact and also the way how sure has been implemented. skills are key. we are in a changing world and this has been accelerated by the pandemic. we have seen digitalisation everywhere, so skills shortages are very much on the digital side and therefore this commission has pushed very much on skills, on the skills side, with the european skills agenda, with a pact for skills. now we will make a proposal on individual learning accounts, and what is also important is the porto objective – 60% of all workers should be part every year of a lifelong learning scheme, and especially i insist on those with the lowest level of skills because their jobs are the most threatened. finally, further work with member states since the very onset of the coronavirus virus outbreak. the commission has coordinated a common european crisis response. we have to come back to the semester. we have to really follow up on what has been decided. we have to analyse the shortcomings in the member states as a consequence of the coronavirus crisis, because we all know that even if this pandemic will end, hopefully soon, we will have the scars of this pandemic. so we have to continue working on these scars through very effective measures.

for the greens/efa group mr president, commissioner, madam, thank you for your report. ladies and gentlemen, whenever we are faced with a crisis, we are also reminded of this uncomfortable truth: the impact of the crisis is extremely uneven. this is also the case with this pandemic we are going through, where the lockdown measures have led to economic recession, mass unemployment, deteriorating working conditions, increased inequality and social exclusion. the statistics are depressing: according to the international labour organization, in the fourth quarter of 2020, the number of working hours worldwide decreased by 4.6%. this represents 130 million full-time jobs and these losses were almost four times as large as during the 2008 financial crisis. to make matters worse, the health and public services of many member states are still affected by austerity measures. these member states are therefore not in a position to provide adequate health services to the population or to protect health and care workers. we should not be concerned only with profits, stability and growth. it is clear that the union will only be able to overcome the crisis and prepare for future crises if we build socially, economically and environmentally more resilient economies. just look around the streets. there are still at least 700 000 people in the union who sleep on the streets or in shelters for the homeless. with the green group we are calling on the commission to take evidence-based action by developing indicators on homelessness and a framework to monitor progress and report back to parliament. we need to do more, and we need to do it now.

member of the commission. – mr president, i will take this oral question on behalf of commissioner kyriakides. we are still in the midst of the pandemic and covid-19 cases remain high across the european union. however, on 31 august, we reached a crucial point in our strategy. we fully vaccinated 70% of the adult population and that number is increasing every day. the eu vaccine strategy, which is an excellent example of eu collaboration, provided a foundation for this progress. i want to thank this parliament for its contributions to that effort over the past year, and i’m delighted to have this opportunity to update you on where we stand. to date, we have signed 10 contracts, seven advance purchase agreements and three purchase agreements with vaccine manufacturers. our negotiations are advanced with one additional company. we continue to work intensively with, and for, member states to ensure a diverse common eu portfolio of vaccines against covid-19 and therapeutics developed using different technologies. the commission fully recognised the importance of transparency in this process. due to the highly competitive nature of this global market, the commission cannot unilaterally disclose all the information contained in the contracts. the law protects the commercial interests of the companies. non-disclosure clauses are a standard feature of purchase agreements. they protect the legitimate interests of the companies, which have made large investment in research and production capacity. there are also rules that protect the tendering process. disclosing sensitive business information would weaken the position of the eu in ongoing negotiations, thus undermining the beneficial effects of fair competition and the effectiveness of the single procurement process which has allowed reaching the best conditions for member states and citizens. but that being said, we have carefully listened to your calls for more transparency and worked with the companies to increase the information that is accessible. until now, we have published nine redacted purchase agreements for covid-19 vaccines. i want to take this opportunity to highlight some crucial points. safety is a fundamental requirement for any vaccine to be deployed in the eu. the safety of citizens is our top priority, and this is non-negotiable. the european medicines agency (ema) has published on its website all trial data of covid-19-related vaccines and therapeutics that are part of marketing authorisation. the agreements with vaccine developers do not change or derogate from the eu legislation and rules on liability. we are acting in full compliance with all applicable rules regarding financial management. the eu vaccine strategy is still being implemented, with new contracts being negotiated and signed. however, further down the line, when we will have a broader perspective, we will conduct an in-depth review and conclude on lessons learned from the strategy. honourable members, i want to conclude with a crucial point. i’m a firm believer of transparency. transparency creates trust, and trust is key when it comes to vaccines. during this process,
