member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i am delighted to join you here today to discuss how we can ensure that all patients have access to affordable, high—quality, safe, sustainable and effective medicines, regardless of where they live in the eu. one year ago, we presented the new pharmaceutical strategy for europe in this house, the strategy that will shape our pharmaceutical policy for the coming decades and aims to ensure that all patients can access the medicines they need, when they need them. this is a fundamental right. it is a crucial part of our collective effort to recover from the pandemic, stronger and more resilient than before. that is why i truly welcome parliament’s report on the pharmaceutical strategy. let me thank the rapporteur, ms montserrat, and the shadow rapporteurs, for this very important work. i am encouraged that you support the objectives of the strategy, but more than that, that you share its ambition. this speaks well for the strategy’s implementation, and in particular the legislative proposals. one of the first deliverables on a flagship initiative is the political agreement on the regulation on health technology assessment (hta), which is expected to be endorsed next month. strengthened eu cooperation on hta will help member states to take more timely and evidence—based decisions on new treatments and improve patients’ access to new innovation. another early deliverable is the health emergency preparedness and response authority (hera), which you have already mentioned and which became operational last month. hera fills a major gap in the eu’s health security framework. it will improve the development, manufacturing, procurement and distribution of key medical countermeasures, including medicines, in the eu. it will push for innovation in areas where we need it most, for example in antibiotics. in addition, the clinical trials regulation becomes applicable in january and will facilitate innovation and more patient—oriented and coordinated development of medicines. political agreement was reached at the end of last month on the strengthened mandate of the european medicines agency to provide for better monitoring and mitigation of shortages, and of critical medicines. to further strengthen the resilience of eu health systems, we have launched a structured dialogue with actors in the pharmaceuticals manufacturing value chain, public authorities, the research community, health professionals and patient organisations to look more strategically at the issue of security of supply. this first phase of the dialogue – the gathering of information – has now been concluded. in the second phase we will reflect on possible policy measures to ensure security of supply of medicines, active pharmaceutical ingredients and raw materials and ultimately reinforce our open strategic autonomy. honourable members, we are still in the early stages of the pharmaceutical strategy, but we are taking concrete steps to deliver real benefits to citizens, and there are more to come. we are working intensively on the evaluation and revision of pharmaceutical legislation. the revision of the legislation on medicines for rare diseases and for children is progressing. both are expected at the end of next year. we are making good progress in implementing the eu strategy on pharmaceuticals in the environment. we have seen that we need stronger supply chains to address dependencies on third countries in terms of raw materials and medicines. before ending i want to stress two important points. firstly, this is about patients. we are putting the patient at the centre of our pharmaceutical policy every step of the way. the overarching goal here is to ensure innovation that addresses unmet medical needs, and this innovation provides affordable medicines that can reach patients across the eu. secondly, this is a complex process, and our success depends on collaboration. we need to work together with all relevant stakeholders, and we are confident that we can. we can reduce shortages of medicines and improve access and affordability; we can support innovation and sustainability in our pharmaceutical sector; and we can deliver concrete, lasting benefits to patients. at the end of the day this translates into healthier citizens, more accessible and more resilient health systems, and stronger and more equal societies. once again, thank you to ms montserrat and all those who contributed to this significant report and i look forward to hearing the discussion.
i would like to thank mrs montserrat and all the rapporteurs from the other groups for the work they have done together over the past few months. i think the final text is really an achievement to be proud of. the pandemic has put health back at the centre and the need for an efficient health and pharmaceutical system that serves the public. with this pharmaceutical strategy, we have restored dignity to patients, and we have called all stakeholders to their responsibilities. on the one hand, there are the public authorities, european and national, who must support research and innovation to enable europe to be independent in the field of health, but on the other hand there are also private actors, who have enormous responsibilities, particularly when they enjoy public financial support and aid. too many countries and too many patients in europe do not have access to treatment, even life-saving treatment, because their markets are not commercially viable. there is still too much discrimination in europe between rich and poor countries. this is no longer acceptable. the time has come to create a true european health union, where all citizens are finally recognised and guaranteed their right to a quality public health system, with minimum standards of health care that are valid throughout europe. we need the utmost transparency and traceability of public funds, with the pricing of medicines not being determined by how important this medicine is to the patients, but being linked to the real costs of research and development. binding marketing clauses are needed in all european countries to ensure that no one is left behind. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the future of the european union's pharmaceutical industry. today is the beginning, not the end, of work on these issues, which are so strategic for the future of our europe.
for the greens/efa group madam president, madam commissioner, i am pleased to see that this report contains some points which are essential for my group. we have finally introduced conditions of transparency, accessibility and affordability for pharmaceutical companies when their products are developed with public funds - funds that come from taxpayers. it is time to put an end to the exorbitant prices charged by pharmaceutical companies, which have also received substantial public subsidies. it is also time to end this system that endangers patients. and i demand that the price of medicines finally reflect the real costs of developing them. at the same time, drug shortages are ravaging some countries in europe and i am pleased to see that the report makes reference to preventing delays in market entry for generics and biosimilars. i am also very concerned about the fact that we finally have stricter obligations in terms of environmental protection and a call for the use of alternatives to animal testing. the medicine is primarily a response to a basic need: health. it should not be in the interests of the pharmaceutical industry alone. so i am still a little disappointed to see references to regulatory flexibility in this report. we need a strong regulatory system to ensure the safety and efficacy of our pharmaceuticals. i am also a little disappointed that this report gives preference to voluntary licensing in the context of a global pandemic, which should allow governments to resort to compulsory licensing in order to meet public health needs. that is why we have tabled an amendment to this paragraph, in order to at least put voluntary licences on an equal footing with compulsory licences. i therefore call on you, ladies and gentlemen, to take responsibility as representatives of the european citizens and to vote in favour of this amendment.
for the left. madam president, i am not at all pleased with the outcome of this report. first, the text has very little ambition in terms of reforming intellectual property rights both inside and outside the european union. after two years of the pandemic, we are still facing the same challenges that we presented five years ago in the resolution on access to medicines in the european union. i believe that patent protection does not and should not serve as a key incentive for innovation. i also believe that compulsory licenses should be used more often. it must be the standard in a pandemic. for this reason, i have tabled an amendment to this report with other colleagues, which incorporates these aspects into the text. secondly, i don't like the way the report puts public support for drug research. if this whole anabasis of vaccine development and supply has taught us anything, it's that the public sector needs to have more control over the subsidies it pays to the pharmaceutical companies. we cannot allow the farce to be repeated again, where vaccines that we paid for with our own money are owned by someone else and we are not allowed to know their price. overall, the ambition to set new rules for transparency in the pharmaceutical sector in terms of pricing, research expenditure and its quantification, and the publication of clinical trial results is grim. for these reasons, i regret that i cannot support the report at this time. it's not bringing anything new.
the pharmaceutical industry and the european health organization have a duty to protect the health of citizens, they have a duty to bring medicines and vaccines to the market. they have a duty to protect the health of citizens from fake medicines and fake vaccines. now it's all coming apart at the seams. the european health organization has approved drugs, or vaccines, against covid, for which there is absolutely no evidence of any efficacy, of 10 percent efficacy. their efficiency is absolutely zero. in countries that are fully vaccinated, the epidemic and the deaths are increasing. in england, there are ten times more cases of covid-19 among those who have been vaccinated and four times more deaths than among those who have not. now is the time to stop this madness and stop the vaccines that aren't.
as a result of past industrial policy, 40% of medicines in the european union today come from third countries. at the same time, 60 to 80% of the active pharmaceutical ingredients are produced in china and india. in such circumstances, further exacerbated by the pandemic, the european union is exposed to the risk of a shortage of medicines. the commission should also be able to provide the european parliament and the council with the information necessary to assess the need for a european pharmaceutical industry. in addition, as a member who has been pushing for the creation of a european health union since the beginning of my mandate, i strongly advocate the extension of joint procurement to include medicines for the treatment of cancer and rare diseases. we will thus strengthen the bargaining position of smaller and less developed member states which do not have the pharmaceutical and industrial capacity of the most developed ones and, of course, achieve price reductions. in this context, the establishment of a european fund for the acquisition of medicines for rare diseases would be of great importance in order to ensure that patients in all member states have equal access to these often very expensive medicines.
the european parliament, by setting up the committee on cancer, has highlighted the importance of this topic for the future of the eu. the union contribution for 2021 amounts to a total of eur 100000000 and is intended to cover the costs of the union contribution for the period from 1 january 2021 to 31 december 2023. these numbers show the enormity of the problem we face. the work of the committee contributes to the creation of a common framework for the treatment of cancer and is an expression of our solidarity, but also of our responsibility for the welfare of citizens throughout the union. in drafting the committee's report, in addition to strengthening prevention, i would highlight as a priority the problem of unequal access to quality cancer treatment. this can be achieved by investing more in european cohesion funds in health infrastructure and workforce, facilitating access to high-quality healthcare in other member states and through joint procurement that will reduce prices and allow all patients in the eu to access the latest medicines on equal terms. we must send a very clear message from here: there can be no first-class and second-class citizens in the european union.
mr president, the croatian public is constantly hearing the story about the huge sums of money from the european funds that have been paid to farmers, and yet there is no serious production or increase in production. in croatia, unfortunately, as much as 70% of eu funds allocated to agriculture and rural development went not to agriculture and rural development but to projects related to the public sector, which does not bring newly created value or jobs that are essential for the existence and survival of the population in rural areas. huge amounts of money were used for non-productive purposes: decorating buildings, squares, offices, or overpaying for software. so we have a case two weeks ago when the former minister of plenkovic's government gabrijela žalac paid for software that cost in real terms not three million kuna, but 13 million kuna. 100% of the funding for this was withdrawn from the competitiveness and cohesion technical support programme. the same was arrested on the basis of a request from the european public prosecutor's office. obviously the main motive for using european funds in croatia is the political survival of the current government, buying the electorate and collecting commissions from contractors that bring huge profits to individuals, as we see. apparently there's a lot of work for the eppo to do.
mr. chairman, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i hadn't planned this, but since it's been mentioned, i'd like to address the issue of the pension first. croatian prosecco and italian prosecco are not the same drink. anyone who says that the croatian past is unfair competition to the italian average is either completely ignorant or malicious. we've heard all sorts of nebulous things about this subject, so i'm taking this opportunity to clarify the situation. now, on the main issues, the common agricultural policy is europe's answer to the need to ensure a decent standard of living for 22 million farmers and a stable, diverse and secure food supply for all european citizens. today, other priorities such as the climate resilience of agricultural production, sustainability and rural development are in focus, as is evident, among other things, by the allocation of 25% of direct payments to eco-schemes. of course, that's commendable, but we must not forget the most important thing, which is to ensure the uninterrupted delivery of quality, healthy food at affordable prices. the covid-19 pandemic has further highlighted the importance of self-sufficiency in food production. the reforms we are implementing and those that will be implemented, based on a farm-to-table strategy, must not jeopardise food supplies or lead to significant price increases. as the epp rapporteur for the imco opinion, i can say that there is a real danger of this happening. farmers were and remain the most important link in the food supply chain. we must see them as partners, not as enemies or obstacles to future development. i think this is a good compromise, although it's not ideal, and i hope that such rational politics will prevail in the future instead of ideology, green or any other.
rapporteur. – mr president, after several months of formal and informal talks and then the very intense conciliation period, i’m really delighted that we have the budget act, which is the basis of the financing for all the european programmes and agencies in the next year. over the course of this year we have echoed a number of political commitments to direct the budget towards a real, tangible instrument tailored to support businesses, regions and generations of citizens. the objective was clear: deliver a strong recovery budget in a year when it is the first time that the annual budget is significantly complemented by the recovery investment plan. in this context, i would really like to thank the parliament delegation, including the shadow rapporteurs and the negotiating team, and also the specialised committees, but also the commission and the council presidency. parliament defended its priorities and secured significant increases in key programmes. an increase of eur 100 million for horizon europe will strengthen research and innovation, and support for small and medium enterprises, with a special allocation for tourism, will speed up the recovery. reinforcement in the life programme will help to achieve a sustainable and energy-efficient economy, and additional funds for erasmus+ will provide thousands of new opportunities for young people. increase in eu4health will enable us to better tackle cross-border health threats, and more resources for the asylum and migration fund, but also for a border and visa management instrument, will help us tackle escalating migratory challenges at our external borders. it was impossible for us to ignore other global developments such as the humanitarian crisis and the need to fight the pandemic outside of europe as well. policies. it was impossible to solve this throughout the annual budgetary procedure, as, similarly, it was impossible to commit on the financing costs of the recovery instrument in the coming years. draft budget for recovery, for research, for health and for other parliament priorities. this would really not be possible without your support and valuable input and once again i really want to thank you all for this.
it is high time that the whole of europe became aware of the worrying, dramatic and dangerous institutional crisis and instability in bosnia and herzegovina. state institutions are paralyzed, society is divided, and electoral engineering has allowed all peoples to be illegitimately represented. the main generators of instability are clear and they feed off each other. separatism on one side and unitarianism on the other. radical centrifugal and centripetal approaches are destroying the foundations of dayton, its fragile peace and coexistence. the solution must start from full respect for the equality of constituent peoples. without electoral reform on such a basis, there will be no functioning european bosnia and herzegovina. my fellow citizens, half a million european citizens live in bosnia and herzegovina. we have a political, but we also have a human responsibility to ensure that their rights are no longer being shamelessly violated.

madam president, i ask myself, what year is it? are we really talking about a year and a half of temporary relaxation of intellectual property protection rules in relation to covid-19 vaccines and drugs on wto grounds? we're breaking it down from left to right, while thousands of people are dying around the world and we're happily creating new mutations. we simply have to ask the question: what is the point of an international treaty that we cannot, for a year and a half, adequately respond to, even the greatest health crisis in a century? why would you do that? so our pharmaceutical companies don't lose their profits? so that a few rich people can get richer? who decides how this world works? state officials or multinational corporations? so let's answer that question. i'm not sure i'm sure anymore. even a small child today must be clear that without giving the rest of the world the chance to be vaccinated, we simply cannot get out of this crisis. let's do something about it and not just talk about it.
dear chairman, dear colleagues, dear citizens, we are here today to discuss how to vaccinate the whole world. covid vaccines have brought us a side effect that has never been seen before in medicine or sociology. a side effect that has never been seen in any vaccine in history. it's a side effect of panic fear. a significant number of vaccinated people are afraid that the unvaccinated will infect them with the covid-19 disease they have been vaccinated against, and they want to vaccinate the whole world. i have to disappoint them. that's not possible. because to do that, everyone has to be vaccinated at the same second or at least the same minute, because otherwise you'd have fully vaccinated again with the 13th booster dose and completely unvaccinated with the 12th booster dose, and those with the 13th would have a legitimate fear of those with the 12th, who could re-infect the vaccinated.
dear president, dear commissioner, dear minister, colleagues, the covid-19 pandemic is a global challenge. it is a global challenge. we'll leave it behind only when all the countries have conquered it. the european union has been leading the effort to get vaccines to developing countries. we must maintain and strengthen these efforts and we must urge our partners and allies to do the same. vaccines have clearly demonstrated their effectiveness in reducing hospitalizations and deaths. the european union's support for the logistics involved in the vaccination processes, particularly from the african continent, is also crucial and the european union is well placed to provide such support. it already has infrastructure for development aid, in particular in the scientific research partnership on aids, malaria and tuberculosis. this partnership has allowed for the creation of capabilities that could be very useful in this battle against the coronavirus. without ensuring adequate conditions on the ground, the simple delivery of vaccines loses its effectiveness.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the recent coronavirus numbers have made it clear that we can only be truly safe if everyone is safe. and a 4% vaccination rate in africa is not acceptable, so we have to do more here. there is a whole bunch of measures we have to take. i call on the council and the member states to release the promised doses for other countries. we are far from having achieved the scale that has been promised. second, of course, we need more money for covax, so that more can be bought. but above all we must increase production capacity. and that means - and i think that is good, commissioner - that we are also supporting the hub in a number of african countries with team europe, so that production capacity can be created there. but what i cannot understand at all is that the companies which are making a lot of money on the cans are not transferring technology and not supporting production. i've looked at all the contracts. there is no obligation to transfer technology, there is no obligation to set up production facilities in developing countries. that needs to change. those who make a lot of money from medicine must also take responsibility and invest in other countries so that vaccines can be produced there too.
madam president, we are all equal, but citizens of the northern hemisphere are a little more equal than those of the southern hemisphere. indeed, if there was any evidence of discrimination against developing countries, the current covid-19 pandemic would have revealed it. there is a gap, a gulf, in access to vaccines between rich and poor countries. the covax mechanism is still doing too little, but what it is doing, it is doing well, very well indeed, especially under the impetus of the european union. and we know why. in the short term, it has been impossible to meet the expectations of developing countries. the problem is structural, we know that. this is why international action is still limited to the humanitarian dimension. for the time being, this approach must be maintained, while more structural approaches are developed in parallel. this must involve a transfer of technical capacity that could enable the production of vaccines on an industrial scale. this must also be done by transferring knowledge to developing countries. this commitment was made by the rich countries in doha 20 years ago. we have to keep that promise. if this fails, we will be in the same situation as today in the next pandemic, with citizens of our countries already receiving the third dose of the vaccine, while more than 90% of the population in the south is still waiting for the first shot.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, this is a serious time. as we debate this, several member states have decided to apply repressive, discriminatory and unconstitutional measures against their people in order to accelerate mass vaccination, without any respect for vaccine freedom. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem, such as the dutch police force, which was ordered last week to shoot the population with live ammunition. discriminatory measures, such as when states such as the czech republic, germany, austria or slovakia decide to confine their unvaccinated under heavy penalties or even imprisonment. unconstitutional measures, such as when the latvian government forbids parliamentarians from voting or participating in debates if they have not received the vaccine. these unquestionable, totally disproportionate measures, which have no scientific basis, are a disgrace to europe, especially when it is proven that the vaccine does not prevent infection with the virus or its transmission. i take as an example our french prime minister, jean castex, who is double vaccinated, who accuses the unvaccinated of all ills, while he himself does not respect any of the barrier gestures, not even the wearing of the mask, and ends up being contaminated by covid. what a scoundrel! madam president, the 66% of the population fully vaccinated in the eu contrasts with 42% globally, or worse, 7% in africa. it was the people who paid for the massive public funds that were mobilized for research, production, early purchase of vaccines. multinational corporations have taken ownership and are raking in profits at the expense of global health, at the expense of public research and production. by preventing the suspension of patents and the possibilities for more rapid progress in the production, distribution and application of vaccines, the eu continues its stubborn defence of the interests of multinationals. contributing to the necessary advancement of vaccination worldwide requires, not a welfare-based approach, but the advocacy of effective development cooperation that includes support for the development of production capacities of the developing countries, free from the logic of multinationals or conditional support strategies. we have said it before and we will not tire of repeating it: vaccines are a public good that should be in the service of humanity!
madam president, when we approach medical products we always look at cost and benefit. i was personally present on 25 february this year in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) in brussels when the ceos of seven big pharmaceutical companies claimed that they have 95% effectiveness, and some even more. nine months later, we can see that this is much less and shorter than in their self-made clinical trials, as highly vaccinated countries have a high number of positive cases, unfortunately. that is benefit, and when it comes to costs, there have been more side effects reported for covid vaccines in one year than for all other vaccines combined in several decades. we have zero data on long-term effects. instead of demanding accountability from those companies for manipulating data regarding their products of questionable quality, the commission and the governments have decided to blame those who haven’t even taken the product for the product failure, attacking them with segregation, fear and guilt campaigns. and now, today, in november 2021, we have come from extremely effective to fourth and fifth doses in some countries. even if you had an elixir of eternal youth, you cannot force it on anyone, let alone experimental products like this.
dear vice president, commissioner, ministers, colleagues and colleagues, i take this opportunity to once again call on all our fellow citizens to place their trust in science and not in those who want to manipulate vulnerable groups of society with false information. those who spread false information, and we heard them here today, unfortunately, are responsible for the deaths of people who refuse to be vaccinated because of their lies. vaccination is the most effective public health measure, and it not only protects us and our loved ones, but it also relieves the burden on the health care system. this allows those in need to receive timely medical care. on the other hand, by not vaccinating, we risk introducing a new strict lockdown that is in no one's interest. let's be clear: the alternative to vaccination and covid certificates is lockdown. that's got to be clear to everyone. the european union is based on solidarity, it does not stop at its borders. we are the world's largest exporter of vaccines, exporting every other vaccine produced in the eu to third countries. by vaccinating the rest of the world, we're reducing the clutch of the virus that could lead to the creation of new, even more infectious strains. it's important to note that it's thanks to intellectual property rights that we've developed a safe and effective vaccine in record time that we're now able to share with the rest of the world. their protection must not be compromised under any circumstances, for without them there can be no medical innovation that we so desperately need. covid-19 is an unpredictable disease that requires a joint coordinated response based on solidarity. the european union has long recognised this, and now it is time for the other world powers to join us in solidarity.

mr president, commissioner, on the occasion of the international day for the elimination of violence against women, i would first like to express my regret at the fact that there are no relevant and up-to-date statistics on this problem. it means that the numbers we have do not reflect the reality that is even worse today. and the statistics are now: one in three women has been a victim of physical or sexual violence in her lifetime, one in four young women has been exposed to domestic violence and intimate partner violence. on average, two out of three women never reported violence to the police. the conclusion we can draw from this is that the problem of violence against women with age is not diminishing but, unfortunately, is growing. existing prevention and protection programs are not working. the low percentage of violence reports is a sign of distrust of institutions. and best of all, these numbers show us very clearly that our key document, the istanbul convention, has not lived up to the way we envisioned and that it is time to take bolder steps. it is our responsibility to monitor the implementation of the istanbul convention, and we expect the european commission to issue a new directive to combat violence against women, promised by the end of this year. i want to believe that the commission will not leave women on the sidelines.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, organized crime groups have traditionally been present in the western balkans as well. they are driven by illicit profit-making where along with corruption they represent one of the greatest threats to the rule of law and prosperity for citizens. at the same time, organised crime in the countries of south-eastern europe is often a source, or at least a transit zone, for crime in the european union. because organised crime knows no borders and this is a debate and a discussion about the security of the whole european union. illegal markets, drug and arms trafficking, corruption at all levels and in all sectors, and money laundering are the main manifestations of the problem. however, we must not forget the most cynical and darkest form of organised crime that has flourished in south-eastern europe in recent years: smuggling and trafficking in human beings. it has unfortunately become one of the most profitable criminal activities but also a means of pressure on european borders. the historical links between organized crime and the yugoslav regime are also well known but much is still hidden. i therefore welcome the call to open all the archives of the former yugoslavia and in particular to provide access to the files of the former yugoslav secret services. in all these areas, the western balkan countries need to show more concrete results in order to make progress on the path towards the european union. we need better security cooperation, the exchange of security and intelligence information and a stronger consideration of the performance of countries in this area in the context of enlargement policy priorities. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the criminal law is applied in a fair and transparent manner.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, fellow citizens of the european union, without employers there is no workforce, without workers there is no employers, without both there is no capitalism. capitalism in a modern society, in a society where human rights are respected, the rights of each other, demands respect from employees and employers. the employer's relationship with the employee includes pay, fair wages for work done. however, what have frau merkel and monsieur macron done to the workers of the european union? they brought millions of immigrants who now work for a much lower wage. many workers have lost their jobs and will continue to lose their jobs unless the wave of migration is stopped. there will be riots all over europe.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are able to enjoy the benefits of the european union's cultural heritage. with a two-millennium-old tradition of celebration, christmas has become much more than a religious holiday on our continent. centuries and centuries of christian culture have shaped europe with christian ideas and values that are at the foundation of european civilization, even when we are not aware of it ourselves. such an identity and rich tradition should not be a source of shame, but on the contrary, a source of pride. so the suggestions to avoid the very memory of christmas, to erase it, or to somehow rename it a holiday season are absurd. christmas has been embedded in the heart of our continent reminding us to stop, to open our hearts to our fellow human beings, especially today in times of great social crisis. to wish christmas in 2021 is to wish the person opposite us all the best and therefore to delete, to avoid wishing christmas for the sake of faded and transient social experiments would be to renounce the europe we know and love, as well as to renounce ourselves. so i wish you a merry and blessed christmas and all the best.
the positive effects of the recovery and resilience facility are slowly but surely already being seen in the economies of the european union and the member states. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's new financial framework and its implementation in the member states. the national recovery and resilience plan supports the reforms that croatia needs and also supports the preparations for the introduction of the euro as an official currency. one of the indicators of positive economic developments is the fact that we are projected to be one of the three fastest growing european economies in the next two years, as well as the most favourable credit rating ever recently granted to the youngest member state of the european union. croatia is facing a year of positive change, preparing for the introduction of the euro and entry into schengen. this is largely possible because of the recovery and resilience mechanism. here in parliament we need to support such success stories, but also to ensure accountability, control and the sound use of taxpayers' money. parliament must fully fulfil its budgetary role, its supervisory role, protect the rule of law and ensure that funds go where they are needed most and where they will make the biggest difference in reducing economic disparities between member states.
author. mr president, in recent years serbia has implemented neoliberal reforms to attract foreign investment and in response to european demands in the context of the accession process. reforms that, as civil society complains, have affected the labour rights of workers with particularly serious cases that the serbian government has tried to hide, such as the linglong factory. but reforms have also devalued serbian environmental regulations. we cannot forget that serbia is the most polluted country in europe. in recent weeks, mass mobilizations have been taking place against the plundering of the country's natural resources. one of the companies criticized in these demonstrations is rio tinto and its jadar project, one of the largest lithium extraction projects that will mainly supply the european market and that will devastate the local environment. it is a coincidence that the resolution did not want to mention this. labour and environmental rights must be defended irrespective of the nationality of the company that violates them. enough of hypocrisy and looking the other way when europe is being tarnished and the shame of its impossible green capitalism is being laid bare. because we don't have a planet b. it is urgent to change the system so as not to change the climate.
on behalf of the ppe club. honourable chairman, commissioner, colleagues, in the last few weeks we have witnessed large environmental protests in serbia where tens of thousands of citizens are participating in road blockades and demanding the abolition of the expropriation act, amendments to the referendum act to prevent the exploitation of lithium in that country. peaceful protests have been followed by a series of incidents and provocations, sporadic clashes, push-backs and fights provoked by groups of provocateurs and supporters of the authorities, with a conspicuous absence of uniformed police. it is clear that the ruling serbian progressive party is threatening political rights and civil liberties in this country. also, the serbian government is under great pressure over the persecution of several hundred vietnamese workers involved in the construction of a chinese automobile tire factory in that country. there are serious indications that vietnamese workers are working in inhuman and unhygienic conditions, but also that they are in serbia illegally, that is, that there are elements of a criminal offence of trafficking in human beings, which is very worrying. in the wider context of the situation in serbia, i would point out the fact that the accession negotiations for serbia's accession to the european union are stagnating. the two main reasons for the lack of progress in opening new chapters are the state of democracy, the rule of law and the slowness of reforms. there has even been a setback on issues crucial to eu accession such as fundamental human rights, the fight against corruption, media independence and the development of civil society, and no concrete results and visible efforts are seen in these areas. in fact, if we look at the situation with media freedom we can see that the situation in that segment is worse than ever. in this way, the serbian government is sending a message that it is not sure if it wants to continue its path towards european integration at all, and it is becoming increasingly clear that president vučić has never really been true to european ideas and values. serbia cannot join the eu until it has resolved fundamental rule of law issues, including the prosecution of war crimes, the fate of missing persons from the aggression against croatia, and the protection of minority rights. its leadership simply has to face these problems and show by deeds, not words, that it has won european values.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, we have tolerated too much of threats from within the union and, above all, from outside it. we can no longer ignore the flagrant violations of the values that have brought us together by members or those who wish to become members of our family. the time for discussion is over. we need concrete action with real results. fake news and anti-european propaganda, hate speech, discrimination and violence, restrictions on freedom of the press, restrictions on freedom of movement, on labour rights, modern slavery - all of this is happening on our soil and at our borders, all of it is being perpetrated and financed by entities with a clear interest in destabilising the union. we must treat them as they deserve: as threats to the security and prosperity of the european citizens who sent us here. we cannot be leaders on the continent and in the world by communicating from warm offices in response to repeated, organized, unsanctioned attacks. to europeans, we are not diplomats, we are just absent, and the enemies dare to shout even louder.
president. - serbia is becoming a chinese colony with chinese factories and chinese working conditions. serbia also has strategic raw materials and is therefore important for the eu. by blocking the accession of serbia and the other western balkan states, the dutch and, more importantly, the french governments are giving china every opportunity to gain a strategic position in europe. china is the source of human rights violations in europe. but we must also take into account the strategic and economic aspects of our position on the admission of the western balkan countries. the situation in the western balkans, through the neglect of the eu, constitutes a major and urgent threat to the economy and political stability of europe. this can even lead to war and large flows of refugees. i therefore call on the european commission and the european council to take action and to start the accession process of the western balkan countries as soon as possible.
mr. chairman, lithium mines are planned at 20 locations in serbia and the key is the jadra valley which has 18 thousand inhabitants with a prominent agriculture and confirms the sad neocolonial reality of europe and china. during angela merkel's recent visit to her epp party colleague vucic, he was a waiter and serbia a buffet. merkel says europe is very interested in serbian lithium. why is europe not interested in its own lithium, when the largest reserves are in germany and the czech republic? because it follows a neo-colonial concept where profit and what's good goes to the center and pollution goes to the periphery. imagine if lithium was found under a vineyard around bordeaux, would the french be talking about displacing the people who live there? if it was found in tuscany, if it was found in the rhine valley, of course it wouldn't. it is not acceptable that the peripheral countries, whether they are members of europe or not, should be a dumping ground for someone to drive electric cars. also, as many as six new thermal power plants are planned for serbia while all over europe, they are shutting down. what is forbidden in europe, when it comes to the environment, cannot be allowed in serbia and bosnia or albania, which also has lithium deposits, and which merkel visited immediately after her visit to belgrade.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we know that the covid crisis has unfortunately disrupted european transport and mobility, especially because of the many restrictions at our internal borders. of course, transport and tourism are the most affected sectors of all, but supply chains are also crucial to the functioning of the internal market in the european union, and we see many, many examples of how they are having a very difficult time today. what solution do i think we're all working on here? it is, of course, a coordinated non-discriminatory approach by the member states. the commission also found that the green belts at border crossing points are not a sufficient means of transport for the goods concerned. i know very well what mileage columns look like because i come from croatia, from the tourist istria, which is unfortunately not yet a member of the schengen area. so after eight years in the waiting room, so to speak, i am looking forward to the further integration of the republic of croatia into the schengen area, or entering it. this is of course a win-win situation for croatia, but also for all member states, and in particular for the many workers, students, drivers, tourists or all the three and a half million people who move across internal borders every day. so, commissioner, i think we need to agree on two points, or two conclusions here today. we need uniform criteria for entry into the member states, that is to say, a european coordinated approach based on the principles of, of course, the common market and non-discrimination, and the full meaning of the schengen area will be given to our europe if all the countries in the waiting room enter it as soon as possible.
